under a pump at the fundamental frequency
\omega_{1}
. The constraints are the power conservation laws at the two frequencies, Eqs. (
F.7
,
F.8
), as well as the conservation of power flow between the two frequencies, Eq. (
F.9
). With
p^{(\text{L})}_{1}
,
p^{(\text{NL})}_{1}
,
p^{(\text{L})}_{2}
,
p^{(\text{NL})}_{2}
as optimization variables, we have

\displaystyle\underset{p^{(\text{L})}_{1},p^{(\text{NL})}_{1},p^{(\text{L})}_{2},p^{(\text{NL})}_{2}}{\text{maximize}}

\displaystyle\omega_{2}\operatorname{Im}(p_{2}^{\dagger}\mathbb{G}_{0,2}p_{2})

(F.12)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\omega_{1}\operatorname{Im}(p^{(\text{NL})\dagger}_{1}\xi_{1}p^{(\text{L})}_{1})=\omega_{1}\operatorname{Im}(e_{\text{inc},1}^{\dagger}p_{1})-\omega_{1}\operatorname{Im}(p_{1}^{\dagger}\mathbb{G}_{0,1}p_{1})-\omega_{1}\operatorname{Im}(p^{(\text{L})\dagger}_{1}\xi_{1}p^{(\text{L})}_{1})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\omega_{2}\operatorname{Im}(p^{(\text{NL})\dagger}_{2}\xi_{2}p^{(\text{L})}_{2})=\omega_{2}\operatorname{Im}(p_{2}^{\dagger}\mathbb{G}_{0,2}p_{2})+\omega_{2}\operatorname{Im}(p^{(\text{L})\dagger}_{2}\xi_{2}p^{(\text{L})}_{2})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\omega_{2}\operatorname{Im}(p^{(\text{NL})\dagger}_{2}\xi_{2}p^{(\text{L})}_{2})=\omega_{1}\operatorname{Im}(p^{(\text{NL})\dagger}_{1}\xi_{1}p^{(\text{L})}_{1}).

This is a QCQP with three constraints, which can be upper bounded via semi-definite relaxation
[
330
]
, as in the computational bound in Chapter
3
. The bound represents the maximal possible efficiency of the second-harmonic generation.

(C) Maximal coupling under specific nonlinear susceptibility

The two previous formulations neglect the underlying nonlinear susceptibility; their bounds are general but may be loose for common nonlinear materials. To include the nonlinear susceptibility
d_{\rm eff}
, we consider the definition of the nonlinear polarization fields:
p^{(\text{NL})}_{1}(\bm{x})=4d_{\rm eff}e^{*}_{1}(\bm{x})e_{2}(\bm{x})
and
p^{(\text{NL})}_{2}(\bm{x})=2d_{\rm eff}e^{2}_{1}(\bm{x})
. Replacing the electric fields with polarization fields via the constitutive relation
e_{i}(\bm{x})=\xi_{i}p^{(\text{L})}_{i}(\bm{x})
, we obtain additional quadratic constraints on the polarization fields. Including these constraints into Problem (
F.12
) yields

\displaystyle\underset{p^{(\text{L})}_{1},p^{(\text{NL})}_{1},p^{(\text{L})}_{2},p^{(\text{NL})}_{2}}{\text{maximize}}

\displaystyle\omega_{2}\operatorname{Im}(p_{2}^{\dagger}\mathbb{G}_{0,2}p_{2})

(F.13)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\omega_{1}\operatorname{Im}(p^{(\text{NL})\dagger}_{1}\xi_{1}p^{(\text{L})}_{1})=\omega_{1}\operatorname{Im}(e_{\text{inc},1}^{\dagger}p_{1})-\omega_{1}\operatorname{Im}(p_{1}^{\dagger}\mathbb{G}_{0,1}p_{1})-\omega_{1}\operatorname{Im}(p^{(\text{L})\dagger}_{1}\xi_{1}p^{(\text{L})}_{1})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\omega_{2}\operatorname{Im}(p^{(\text{NL})\dagger}_{2}\xi_{2}p^{(\text{L})}_{2})=\omega_{2}\operatorname{Im}(p_{2}^{\dagger}\mathbb{G}_{0,2}p_{2})+\omega_{2}\operatorname{Im}(p^{(\text{L})\dagger}_{2}\xi_{2}p^{(\text{L})}_{2})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\omega_{2}\operatorname{Im}(p^{(\text{NL})\dagger}_{2}\xi_{2}p^{(\text{L})}_{2})=\omega_{1}\operatorname{Im}(p^{(\text{NL})\dagger}_{1}\xi_{1}p^{(\text{L})}_{1}).

\displaystyle p^{(\text{NL})}_{1}(\bm{x})=4d_{\rm eff}\xi_{1}^{*}\xi_{2}p^{(\text{L})*}_{1}(\bm{x})p^{(\text{L})}_{2}(\bm{x}),\quad{\rm for\ every\ }\bm{x}

\displaystyle p^{(\text{NL})}_{2}(\bm{x})=2d_{\rm eff}\xi_{1}^{2}p^{(\text{L})2}_{1}(\bm{x}),\quad{\rm for\ every\ }\bm{x}.

This optimization problem is a QCQP, again amendable to global bounds via semidefinite relaxation. The resulting bound, incorporating the specific nonlinear coefficient of the material, is the tightest bounds for nonlinear materials among all three we have discussed so far.

(D) Perturbation limit

If the pump is weak, the nonlinear polarization can be treated as a perturbation, so that
p_{1}(\bm{x})\approx p^{(\text{L})}_{1}(\bm{x})\gg p^{(\text{NL})}_{1}(\bm{x})
.
For simplicity, we choose the objective as
\int_{V}|p^{(\text{NL})}_{2}(\bm{x})|^{2}\text{d}\bm{x}
to characterize the amount of nonlinear generation at
\omega_{2}
. Considering the nonlinear coupling relation
p^{(\text{NL})}_{2}(\bm{x})=2d_{\rm eff}p^{(\text{L})2}_{1}(\bm{x})
and the relation in the perturbation limit
p^{(\text{L})}_{1}(\bm{x})\approx p_{1}(\bm{x})
, the objective function can be tranformed into a function of
p_{1}(\bm{x})
, which reads
4|d_{\rm eff}|^{2}\int_{V}|p_{1}(\bm{x})|^{4}\text{d}\bm{x}.
With this new objective function, problem (
F.13
) reduces to:

\displaystyle\underset{p_{1}}{\text{maximize}}

\displaystyle\int_{V}\left|p_{1}(\bm{x})\right|^{4}\text{d}\bm{x}

(F.14)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}(e_{\text{inc},1}^{\dagger}p_{1})=\omega_{1}\operatorname{Im}(p^{\dagger}_{1}\mathbb{G}_{0,1}p_{1})+\omega_{1}\operatorname{Im}(p^{\dagger}_{1}\xi_{1}p_{1}).

In the perturbation limit, the power conservation constraint degenerates to its linear counterpart, with a fourth-order objective function of the polarization field
p_{1}
. This is a quartic optimization problem, which also has known computational upper bounds
[
77
]
.

F.4 Conclusion

In this appendix, we generalize our power conservation laws to nonlinear nanophotonics by considering additional nonlinear polarization fields. The generalized conservation laws promise bounds on maximal nonlinear generations.
In the context of second-harmonic generation, we propose four upper bounds for increasingly more concrete scenarios. First, we show an analytically solvable upper bound in Problem (
F.11
) that determines the maximal nonlinear coupling out of a given frequency. Then, we establish a tighter computational bound in Problem (
F.12
) by including potential dissipation at the second frequency. Both Problems (
F.11
) and (
F.12
) solve for the maximal nonlinear conversion out of all possible nonlinear materials.
For a given nonlinear material, we formulate the tightest bound in Problem (
F.13
), and simplify it in the perturbation limit to Problem (
F.14
).

Problems (
F.13
,
F.14
) are difficult to solve for designs beyond the wavelength scale due to their many constraints. The former, Problem (
F.13
), is a QCQP with
2N+3
constraints for
N
number of descritized spatial points. The latter, Problem (
F.13
), is a quartic problem which is equivalent to a QCQP with at least
N
constraints
[
77
]
. Both problems suffer from the same O(
N^{4}
) complexity as the computational bound in Chapter
3
. To solve them efficiently, we can replace the constraints with their weighted averages as in Chapter
3
. In addition, if the nonlinear design has a “long” dimension, the bounds may be susceptible to the sparse-based acceleration in Chapter
5
.
Both acceleration methods may lead to bounds for nonlinear designs beyond the wavelength scale.

Appendix G
Analytical bounds via optical theorem: supplementary

This appendix provides supplementary information to Chapter
2
, “Analytical bounds via optical theorem.”
We (1) establish our general framework through Lagrangian dual function, (2) derive expressions for bounds on power quantities presented in Section
2.2
, (3) derive bounds in the special case for plane wave incidence presented in Section
2.3
, (4) derive bounds for extended scatters which are greatly simplified for the special case of plane wave incidence, (5) discuss minimum thickness required for perfect absorbers, (6) lay out the inverse design procedure for the ultrathin absorber and (7) the corresponding optimal designs for the data presented in Fig.
2.3
(b,c) and Fig.
2.4
(c), (8) show how our formalism can incorporate previous predicted limits on nanoparticle scattering, thin film absorption, and thermal absorption. We provide real-space expressions for the eigen-expansions of imaginary part of the electric Green’s function operator for (9) a sphere and (10) a planar film. We (11) summarize expressions for upper bounds on three power quantities at different generality, with additional applications for (12) local density of states manifested from near field interaction and (13) thermal absorption and emission from incoherent sources.

G.1 The optimization problem and its dual function

The optimization problem is to maximize a response function
f(p)=p^{\dagger}\mathbb{A}p+\operatorname{Im}\left[\beta^{\dagger}p\right]
under the optical-theorem constraint, where the variable
p
is polarization field induced in the scatterer. Under a prespecified basis, parameter
\beta
is a vector, and
\mathbb{A}
is a Hermitian matrix.
The same basis defines positive semidefinite matrix
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}
and
\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}
, representing radiative and material loss in the system.
Following the standard optimization notation, we rewrite the original maximization problem as a minimization problem by adding a minus sign to the objective function:

\displaystyle\underset{p}{\text{minimize}}

\displaystyle-f(p)=-p^{\dagger}\mathbb{A}p-\operatorname{Im}\left[\beta^{\dagger}p\right]

(G.1)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle p^{\dagger}\left\{\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}\right\}p=\operatorname{Im}\left[e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}p\right].

The optimization problem stated in Eq. (
G.1
) is known to have strong duality
[
118
]
, prompting us to find its dual function, which in turn is defined by its Lagrangian:

L(p,\nu)=p^{\dagger}\mathbb{B}(\nu)p-\operatorname{Im}\left[(\beta+\nu e_{\rm inc})^{\dagger}p\right],

(G.2)

where we introduce dual variable
\nu
and simplify our notation by introducing matrix

\mathbb{B}(\nu)=-\mathbb{A}+\nu(\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}).

(G.3)

The dual function
g(\nu)
is defined as the minimum of Lagrangian
L(\phi,\nu)
over variable
\phi
. We denote
\nu_{0}
as the value of
\nu
when the minimum eigenvalue of
\mathbb{B}(\nu)
is zero, leaving
\mathbb{B}(\nu_{0})
a positive semidefinite matrix with at least one zero eigenvalue. For
\nu<\nu_{0}
, the positivity of
\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}
implies that
\mathbb{B}(\nu)=\mathbb{B}(\nu_{0})-(\nu_{0}-\nu)(\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0})
has negative eigenvalues and
L(\phi,\nu)
is unbounded below. For
\nu>\nu_{0}
,
\mathbb{B}(\nu)=\mathbb{B}(\nu_{0})+(\nu-\nu_{0})(\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0})
is positive definite, and
L(\phi,\nu)
is convex in
\phi
with a finite minimal value. This minimum is obtained at

p(\nu)=\frac{i}{2}\mathbb{B}^{-1}(\nu)(\beta+\nu e_{\rm inc}),

(G.4)

with the resulting dual function:

\displaystyle g(\nu)=\min_{p}L(p,\nu)=\begin{cases}-\frac{1}{4}(\beta+\nu e_{\rm inc})^{\dagger}\mathbb{B}^{-1}(\nu)(\beta+\nu e_{\rm inc})&\nu>\nu_{0}\\
-\infty&\nu<\nu_{0}.\end{cases}

(G.5)

Lastly, at
\nu=\nu_{0}
, if
\beta+\nu_{0}e_{\rm inc}
is in the range of
\mathbb{B}(\nu_{0})
, then
L(p,\nu)
is still convex and
g(\nu_{0})
takes the value of the first case in Eq. (
G.5
) with the inverse operator replaced by the pseudo-inverse; if not, then Eq. (
G.2
) suggests that
L(p,\nu)
is unbounded below and
g(\nu_{0})\rightarrow-\infty
.

Due to strong duality, the optimization problem, Eq. (
G.1
), is solved by finding the maximum of the dual function:

\displaystyle\underset{\nu}{\text{maximize}}

\displaystyle g(\nu).

(G.6)

According to Eq. (
G.5
), dual function
g(\nu)
is maximized at a value within range
[\nu_{0},+\infty)
, which we denote as
\nu^{*}
. The maximum response function takes the (negative of the optimal dual) value:

\displaystyle f_{\rm max}=\frac{1}{4}(\beta+\nu^{*}e_{\rm inc})^{\dagger}\mathbb{B}^{-1}(\nu^{*})(\beta+\nu^{*}e_{\rm inc}),

(G.7)

and the optimal polarization field
p
is given by evaluating Eq. (
G.4
) at
\nu^{*}
:

p^{*}=\frac{i}{2}\mathbb{B}^{-1}(\nu^{*})(\beta+\nu^{*}e_{\rm inc}),

(G.8)

except when
\nu^{*}=\nu_{0}
, where the
p^{*}
can not be uniquely determined due to the presence of zero eigenvalues in
\mathbb{B}(\nu_{0})
.

To solve for the maximum response function
f_{\rm max}
in Eq. (
G.7
), we need to find the optimal dual variable
\nu^{*}
, which can only occur either in the interior of the domain
[\nu_{0},\infty)
or its boundary. If
\nu^{*}
is in the interior, it has to satisfy the condition:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g(\nu)}{\partial\nu}\biggr\rvert_{\nu=\nu^{*}}=0.

(G.9)

This can be translated to a transcendental equation that determines the first possible optimum which we denote as
\nu_{1}
:

\displaystyle 2\operatorname{Re}\left\{e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\mathbb{B}^{-1}(\nu_{1})(\beta+\nu_{1}e_{\rm inc})\right\}

(G.10)

\displaystyle-(\beta+\nu_{1}e_{\rm inc})^{\dagger}\mathbb{B}^{-1}(\nu_{1})

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}^{\prime}(\nu_{1})\mathbb{B}^{-1}(\nu_{1})(\beta+\nu_{1}e_{\rm inc})=0.

The concavity of the dual function
g(\nu)
guarantees the uniqueness of the solution
\nu_{1}
. The lefthand side of Eq. (
G.10
) is proportional to
-\partial g(\lambda)/\partial\nu
. Its derivative,
-\partial^{2}g(\lambda)/\partial\nu^{2}
, is always non-negative based on the second-order condition of a concave function
[
118
]
. Thus, if there is a
\nu_{1}
satisfying Eq. (
G.10
), it can simply be solved by identifying where the sign of the lefthand side changes, using either bisection or Newton’s method.

Based on the concavity of the dual function, we can also argue that if
\nu_{1}
exists in the domain
(\nu_{0},\infty)
then it must be the global optimizer of
g(\nu)
. If not, then there is no point in the domain at which the gradient is zero, and
\nu^{*}
must be one of the boundary values of
[\nu_{0},\infty)
; by the concavity of
g(\nu)
, the maximum must occur at
\nu_{0}
. Hence we have:

\displaystyle\nu^{*}=\begin{cases}\nu_{1}&\textrm{if }\nu_{1}\in(\nu_{0},\infty)\\
\nu_{0}&\textrm{else.}\end{cases}

(G.11)

The self-consistency implicit in Eq. (
G.10
) for
\nu_{1}
can make it to difficult to ascertain whether
\nu_{1}
or
\nu_{0}
is optimal. Instead, if the derivative of
g(\nu)
at
\nu_{0}
is well-defined, we can check its value to determine whether
g(\nu)
attains it extremum in the interior of its domain or on its boundary: if and only if it is positive, then
\nu_{1}
will be in the interior of the domain
[\nu_{0},\infty)
. Hence, if
\beta+\nu_{0}e_{\rm inc}
is in the range of
{\mathbb{B}^{-1}(\nu_{0})}
, then we can also use the equivalent condition to determine
\nu^{*}
:

\displaystyle\nu^{*}=\begin{cases}\nu_{1}&\textrm{if }2e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\mathbb{B}^{-1}(\nu_{0})(\beta+\nu_{0}e_{\rm inc})\\
&\quad\quad<(\beta+\nu_{0}e_{\rm inc})^{\dagger}\mathbb{B}^{-1}(\nu_{0})\mathbb{B}^{\prime}(\nu_{0})\mathbb{B}^{-1}(\nu_{0})(\beta+\nu_{0}e_{\rm inc})\\
\nu_{0}&\textrm{else.}\end{cases}

(G.12)

G.2 Absorbed, scattered, and extinguished power expressions

We start with extinguished power which is linear in polarization field
p
:
P_{\rm ext}=\frac{\omega}{2}\operatorname{Im}\left[e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}p\right]
. For simplicity, we take the objective function as
\operatorname{Im}\left[e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}p\right]
, and set
\mathbb{A}=0
and
\beta=e_{\rm inc}
in the optimization problem, Eq. (
G.1
). The matrix defined in Eq. (
G.3
) becomes
\mathbb{B}(\nu)=\nu(\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0})
. Its minimum eigenvalue reaches zero when
\nu=\nu_{0}=0
. Dual function in Eq. (
G.5
) takes the form:

\displaystyle g(\nu)=\begin{cases}-\frac{(\nu+1)^{2}}{4}e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}(\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0})^{-1}e_{\rm inc}&\nu>0\\
-\infty&\nu\leq 0,\end{cases}

(G.13)

where we identified
g(\nu_{0})=-\infty
since
\beta+\nu_{0}e_{\rm inc}
is not in the range of
B(\nu_{0})
. Since
g(\nu_{0})=-\infty
, the optimal dual variable
\nu^{*}
can only be chosen at
\nu_{1}
. Solving Eq. (
G.10
) gives
\nu_{1}=1
and the maximum extinction given by Eq. (
G.7
) is (after adding back the
\frac{\omega}{2}
prefactor):

\displaystyle P_{\rm ext}^{\rm max}=\frac{\omega}{2}e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\left(\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}\right)^{-1}e_{\rm inc}.

(G.14)

The optimum polarization field
p^{*}
is given by Eq. (
G.8
):

p^{*}=i(\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0})^{-1}e_{\rm inc}.

(G.15)

Absorption has the form
P_{\rm abs}=\frac{\omega}{2}p^{\dagger}(\operatorname{Im}\xi)p
. Taking the objective function as
p^{\dagger}(\operatorname{Im}\xi)p
, we have
\mathbb{A}=\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}
and
\beta=0
in the optimization problem, Eq. (
G.1
). The matrix defined in Eq. (
G.3
) becomes
\mathbb{B}(\nu)=(\nu-1)\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\nu\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}
. Dual function takes the form of Eq. (
G.5
):

\displaystyle g(\nu)=\begin{cases}-\frac{\nu^{2}}{4}e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}[(\nu-1)\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\nu\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}]^{-1}e_{\rm inc}&\nu>\nu_{0}\\
-\infty&\nu<\nu_{0},\end{cases}

(G.16)

At
\nu=\nu_{0}
, the value of
g(\nu_{0})\rightarrow-\infty
if
e_{\rm inc}
is not in the range of
\mathbb{B}(\nu_{0})
, otherwise
g(\nu_{0})
takes the form of the first case in Eq. (
G.16
) with the inverse replaced by pseudo-inverse.
As in Eq. (
G.11
), the optimal dual variable
\nu^{*}
is obtained either at the interval
(\nu_{0},\infty)
or its boundary
\nu_{0}
. The value of
\nu_{0}
depends on the nature of both
\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}
and
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}
. The value of
\nu_{1}
is given by Eq. (
G.10
):

e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\left[2\mathbb{B}^{-1}(\nu_{1})-\nu_{1}\mathbb{B}^{-1}(\nu_{1})(\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0})\mathbb{B}^{-1}(\nu_{1})\right]e_{\rm inc}=0.

(G.17)

Using Eq. (
G.7
) and adding back the
\frac{\omega}{2}
prefactor, we have maximum absorption:

\displaystyle P_{\rm abs}^{\rm max}=\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}[(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}]^{-1}e_{\rm inc}.

(G.18)

The optimal current can be determined by Eq. (
G.8
) in the case of
\nu^{*}=\nu_{1}
:

p^{*}=i\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}[(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}]^{-1}e_{\rm inc}.

(G.19)

Scattering power has the form
P_{\rm scat}=\frac{\omega}{2}p^{\dagger}(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0})p
, such that
\mathbb{A}=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}
and
\beta=0
after suppressing the
\frac{\omega}{2}
prefactor. Following a similar procedure as absorption, we have maximum scattering as:

\displaystyle P_{\rm scat}^{\rm max}=\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}[\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}]^{-1}e_{\rm inc}.

(G.20)

Again,
\nu^{*}
takes two possible values:
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{0}
, as dictated by Eq. (
G.11
). The determinant equation for
\nu_{1}
takes the same form as Eq. (
G.17
) with
\mathbb{B}(\nu)=\nu\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+(\nu-1)\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}
.

An equivalent formulation for all three power quantities is to write them as the difference (or sum) of the other two. For example, scattering power can be written as the difference between extinction and absorption:
P_{\rm scat}=\frac{\omega}{2}\operatorname{Im}\left[e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}p\right]-\frac{\omega}{2}p^{\dagger}(\operatorname{Im}\xi)p
. With
\mathbb{A}=-\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}
and
\beta=e_{\rm inc}
after suppressing the
\frac{\omega}{2}
prefactor, this gives the same optima as in Eq. (
G.20
) but with a different form:

\displaystyle P_{\rm scat}^{\rm max}=\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{(1+\nu^{*})^{2}}{4}e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\left[(\nu^{*}+1)\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}+\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}\right]^{-1}e_{\rm inc},

(G.21)

where the optimal dual variable
\nu^{*}
is determined by Eq. (
G.11
).

G.3 Bounds for a nonmagnetic scalar material under plane wave incidence

Let us consider a typical case where the incident field is a plane wave and the scatterer is composed of nonmagnetic scalar material.
Because
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}
is positive-semidefinite, we can simplify its eigendecomposition to write
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}=\mathbb{V}\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}
, where the columns of
\mathbb{V}
, which we denote
v_{i}
, form an orthogonal basis of polarization fields. They are normalized such that the set
\rho_{i}=v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}
are the eigenvalues of
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}
and represent the powers radiated by unit-normalization polarization fields. The expansion of incident plane wave,
e_{\rm inc}
, in these channels is assumed to be:
e_{\rm inc}=\frac{1}{k^{3/2}}\sum_{i}e_{i}v_{i}
, where the exact value of
|e_{i}|^{2}
depends on the choice of
v_{i}
.

We decompose general bounds given by Eq. (
G.14
,
G.18
,
G.20
) into contributions from these channels:

\displaystyle P_{\rm ext}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{1}{k^{3}}\sum_{i}|e_{i}|^{2}\frac{\rho_{i}}{\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\rho_{i}}

(G.22)

\displaystyle P_{\rm abs}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\frac{1}{k^{3}}\sum_{i}|e_{i}|^{2}\frac{\rho_{i}}{(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\nu^{*}\rho_{i}}

(G.23)

\displaystyle P_{\rm scat}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\frac{1}{k^{3}}\sum_{i}|e_{i}|^{2}\frac{\rho_{i}}{\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+(\nu^{*}-1)\rho_{i}}.

(G.24)

Taking
\omega=k
in our unitless convention and write
k=2\pi/\lambda
gives the expressions presented in Eqs. (
2.8
)–(
2.10
) in Chapter
2
.
Bounds for both absorption and scattering contain
\nu^{*}
, which is determined by Eq. (
G.11
). For absorption,
\nu_{0}=1
, and
\nu_{1}
is computationally evaluated by solving the following equation:

\sum_{i}|e_{i}|^{2}\frac{(\nu_{1}-2)\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\nu_{1}\rho_{i}}{[(\nu_{1}-1)\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\nu_{1}\rho_{i}]^{2}}=0.

(G.25)

For scattering bound,
\nu_{0}=\rho_{\rm max}/(\rho_{\rm max}+\operatorname{Im}{\xi})
, where
\rho_{\rm max}
is the largest
\rho_{i}
. The other potential optimum,
\nu_{1}
, is solved computationally through equation:

\sum_{i}|e_{i}|^{2}\rho_{i}\frac{(\nu_{1}-2)\rho_{i}+\nu_{1}\operatorname{Im}{\xi}}{[(\nu_{1}-1)\rho_{i}+\nu_{1}\operatorname{Im}{\xi}]^{2}}=0.

(G.26)

Bounds on maximal cross sections for a finite-size scatterer is obtained by normalizing Eqs. (
G.22
)–(
G.24
) by plane wave intensity
|E_{0}|^{2}/2
(the vacuum resistance
Z_{0}=1
):

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm ext}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4\pi^{2}|E_{0}|^{2}}\sum_{i}|e_{i}|^{2}\frac{\rho_{i}}{\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\rho_{i}}

(G.27)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm abs}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4\pi^{2}|E_{0}|^{2}}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\sum_{i}|e_{i}|^{2}\frac{\rho_{i}}{(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\nu^{*}\rho_{i}}

(G.28)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm scat}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4\pi^{2}|E_{0}|^{2}}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\sum_{i}|e_{i}|^{2}\frac{\rho_{i}}{\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+(\nu^{*}-1)\rho_{i}}.

(G.29)

For a plane wave incidence with
|e_{i}|^{2}=\pi(2n+1)\delta_{m,\pm 1}|E_{0}|^{2}
, we can simplify the above expression by summing over index
m
within
i=\{m,n,j\}
, leaving contributions indexed only by total angular momentum
n
and polarization state
j
:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm ext}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2\pi}\sum_{n,j}(2n+1)\frac{\rho_{n,1,j}}{\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\rho_{n,1,j}}

(G.30)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm abs}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2\pi}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\sum_{n,j}(2n+1)\frac{\rho_{n,1,j}}{(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\nu^{*}\rho_{n,1,j}}

(G.31)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm scat}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2\pi}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\sum_{n,j}(2n+1)\frac{\rho_{n,1,j}}{\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+(\nu^{*}-1)\rho_{n,1,j}}.

(G.32)

In Fig.
2.2
(c) of Chapter
2
, we use the notation
\sigma_{\rm ext,n}
to denote the contribution from the n-th channel in the summation of Eq. (
G.30
).

G.4 General bound for extended scatterers

In a planar bounding volume for extended scatterers, the most general far-field incidence has the expansion:

e_{\rm inc}=\frac{1}{k^{3/2}}\sum_{i}\int_{k_{\parallel}\leq k}e_{i}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})v_{i}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})\frac{\text{d}\bm{k}_{\parallel}}{(2\pi)^{2}},

(G.33)

where index
i=\{s,p\}
. Plugging the expansion of
e_{\rm inc}
in Eq. (
G.14
,
G.18
,
G.20
) gives the integral form of cross-sections bounds after normalization by the z-directed plane wave intensity
|E_{0}|^{2}k_{z}/2k
:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm ext}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{kk_{z}|E_{0}|^{2}}\sum_{i}\int_{k_{\parallel}\leq k}|e_{i}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})|^{2}\frac{\rho_{i}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})}{\operatorname{Im}\xi+\rho_{i}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})}\frac{\text{d}\bm{k}_{\parallel}}{(2\pi)^{2}}

(G.34)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm abs}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{kk_{z}|E_{0}|^{2}}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\sum_{i}\int_{k_{\parallel}\leq k}|e_{i}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})|^{2}\frac{\rho_{i}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})}{(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}\xi+\nu^{*}\rho_{i}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})}\frac{\text{d}\bm{k}_{\parallel}}{(2\pi)^{2}}

(G.35)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm scat}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{kk_{z}|E_{0}|^{2}}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\sum_{i}\int_{k_{\parallel}\leq k}|e_{i}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})|^{2}\frac{\rho_{i}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})}{\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\xi+(\nu^{*}-1)\rho_{i}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})}\frac{\text{d}\bm{k}_{\parallel}}{(2\pi)^{2}}.

(G.36)

Now we restrict our scope to a plane wave incidence with total wave vector
\bm{k}=k_{x}\bm{\hat{e}}_{x}+k_{y}\bm{\hat{e}}_{y}+k_{z}\bm{\hat{e}}_{z}
and polarization
p^{\prime}
. We denote its parallel wave vector as
\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime}=k_{x}\bm{\hat{e}}_{x}+k_{y}\bm{\hat{e}}_{y}
where the
′
symbol differentiates
\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime}
from
\bm{k}_{\parallel}
that is used to label different channels in Eq. (
G.33
). The plane wave has the expression:

e_{\rm inc}=E_{0}\hat{\bm{e}}e^{i\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime}\cdot\bm{r}_{\parallel}}e^{ik_{z}z},

(G.37)

where
\hat{\bm{e}}
is a unit vector denotes incident polarization, taking the form
(k_{y}\bm{\hat{e}}_{x}-k_{x}\bm{\hat{e}}_{y})/k_{\parallel}^{\prime}
for
p^{\prime}=M
, and
(-k_{z}\bm{\hat{k}^{\prime}_{\parallel}}+k_{\parallel}^{\prime}\bm{\hat{e}}_{z})/k
for
p^{\prime}=N
. Equating Eq. (
G.37
) with Eq. (
G.33
) gives the expansion coefficients,
e_{i}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})
.
Plugging its absolute value
|e_{i}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})|=|E_{0}|\sqrt{2k_{z}k}(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime}-\bm{k}_{\parallel})\delta_{p,p^{\prime}}
into Eqs. (
G.34
)–(
G.36
) gives bounds for plane wave incidence:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm ext}/A

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{s=\pm}\frac{\rho_{s,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})}{\operatorname{Im}\xi+\rho_{s,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})}

(G.38)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm abs}/A

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu^{*2}}{2}\sum_{s=\pm}\frac{\rho_{s,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})}{(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\nu^{*}\rho_{s,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})}

(G.39)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm scat}/A

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu^{*2}}{2}\sum_{s=\pm}\frac{\rho_{s,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})}{\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+(\nu^{*}-1)\rho_{s,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})},

(G.40)

where we identified factor
A=(2\pi)^{2}\delta^{2}(0)
corresponding to total surface area.

Bounds for both absorption and scattering contain
\nu^{*}
, which is determined by Eq. (
G.11
). For absorption,
\nu_{0}=1
, and
\nu_{1}
is computationally evaluated by solving the following equation:

\sum_{s=\pm}\frac{(\nu_{1}-2)\operatorname{Im}\xi+\nu_{1}\rho_{s,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})}{\left[(\nu_{1}-1)\operatorname{Im}\xi+\nu_{1}\rho_{s,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})\right]^{2}}=0.

(G.41)

For scattering,
\nu_{0}=\rho_{\rm max}/(\rho_{\rm max}+\operatorname{Im}{\xi})
, where
\rho_{\rm max}
is the largest
\rho_{i}
. The other potential optimum,
\nu_{1}
, is solved computationally through equation:

\sum_{s=\pm}\rho_{s,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})\frac{(\nu_{1}-2)\rho_{s,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})+\nu_{1}\operatorname{Im}\xi}{\left[(\nu_{1}-1)\rho_{s,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})+\nu_{1}\operatorname{Im}\xi\right]^{2}}=0.

(G.42)

Figure G.1:
Comparison between skin depth and minimum thickness,
h_{\rm min}
, for a perfect absorber as a function of wavelength
\lambda
for a Drude metal. General bound and lossy-material bound are shown seperately in two plots. The former one gives a much more modest prediction.
All quantities are normalized by the plasmonic wavelength,
\lambda_{p}
, of the Drude metal.

G.5 Minimum thickness for perfect absorbers

Following Section
G
, this section studies minimum thickness required for a perfect absorber that has 100% absorption. Usually, one determines the optimal
\nu^{*}
in Eq. (
G.39
) by comparing the values of
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
. Here, we take an alternative approach introduced through Eq. (
G.12
), where the derivative of the dual function at
\nu_{0}
is used as a threshold, giving a explicit expression for maximum absorption cross section:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm abs}/A\leq\begin{cases}\frac{\nu_{1}^{2}}{2}\sum_{s=\pm}\frac{\rho_{s,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})}{(\nu_{1}-1)\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\nu_{1}\rho_{s,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})},&\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}{\xi}}>\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{1}{\rho_{+,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})}+\frac{1}{\rho_{-,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})}\right]\\
1,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(G.43)

Threshold for maximum absorption (at a given incident angle) corresponds to the condition:

\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}{\xi}}=\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{1}{\rho_{+,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})}+\frac{1}{\rho_{-,p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})}\right],

(G.44)

where we can solve for its required minimum thickness:

h_{\rm min}=\frac{k_{z}}{k^{2}}\frac{4\operatorname{Im}{\xi}}{1-\text{sinc}^{2}(k_{z}h_{\rm min})}.

(G.45)

Under normal incidence (
k_{z}=k
), when the absorber is much thinner than the wavelength,
kh_{\rm min}\rightarrow 0
, it can be shown that:

kh_{\rm min}=(24\operatorname{Im}{\xi})^{1/3}.

(G.46)

This prodicts a much more modest improvement over reduced material loss, compared with previous lossy-material bound
[
61
]
where the expression for minimum thickness under normal incidence is
kh_{\rm min}=\operatorname{Im}\xi
. Fig.
G.1
shows that this contrast is on the order of
10^{2}
for a Drude metal modeled by permittivity

\varepsilon(\omega)=-\frac{\omega_{p}^{2}}{\omega^{2}+i\gamma\omega},

(G.47)

with loss rate
\gamma=0.02\omega_{p}
. Plasmonic wavelength is
\lambda_{p}=2\pi c/\omega_{p}
, with
c=1
being the speed of light in our unitless convention.

It is also shown in Fig.
G.1
that, minimum thickness predicted by the general bound is on the same length scale as skin depth in the metal
[
119
]
near plasmonic wavelength,
\lambda_{p}
. For
\lambda<\lambda_{p}
, there is no surface plasmonic mode inside a Drude metal and skin depth is ill-defined, though it is still possible to realize a perfect absorber according to the general bound. For
\lambda>\lambda_{p}
, while both skin depth and lossy-material bound reach a plateau at large wavelength limit, general bound has
h_{\text{min}}
increases proportionally to wavelength. This comes from the effectively thinner material under large wavelength incidence and explains the behavior of Al in Fig.
2.4
(a) of Chapter
2
.

G.6 Inverse design procedures for perfect absorbers

In Fig.
2.3
(b,c) and Fig.
2.4
(c) of Chapter
2
, we showed examples of maximum absorption of topology-optimized metasurfaces with subwavelength periodicity, which are generally within
70\%
of the bounds, and therefore confirming our bounds to be tight or nearly so. Here we present the details of the topology optimization procedures. Given the permittivity of the material
\varepsilon_{\rm m}
, using a material density function
\alpha_{i}
, with the subscript
i
standing for its spatial coordinate,
\alpha_{i}=1
meaning material and
\alpha_{i}=0
meaning air at pixel
i
, then the design problem of perfect absorbers is formulated as a maximization of the absorption cross section over all permissible choice of
\alpha_{i}
at each pixel
i
:

\displaystyle\underset{\varepsilon_{i}}{\text{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm abs}

(G.48)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}=1+\alpha_{i}(\varepsilon_{\rm m}-1),

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\in[0,1],

and the absorption cross section as function of the six-vector field
\psi
is given by
\sigma_{\rm abs}(\psi)=\frac{1}{A}\int_{V}\frac{\omega}{2}\psi^{\dagger}\frac{\operatorname{Im}\chi}{|\chi|^{2}}\psi
, where
A
is the unit cell area. The Maxwell constraint, i.e. that all solutions satisfy Maxwell’s equations is implied.

Global optimization methods tend not to provide reasonable convergences with such large dimensionality of the problem. Hence local optimizations with random initial starting points were tested to approach the global bounds. Fast calculations of the gradients
\partial\sigma_{\rm abs}/\partial\alpha_{i}
are facilitated with the adjoint method
[
26
]
. Following the volume-integral formalism, one can take the variation of any generic figure of merit
f(\psi)
due to changes in the susceptibility
\Delta\chi
:

\displaystyle\delta f

\displaystyle=2\operatorname{Re}\int_{V}\left(\delta\psi\right)^{\rm T}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\psi}.

(G.49)

Considering that the perturbed field, expressed with six-vector Green’s function
\Gamma_{0}(x,x^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle\delta\psi(x)=\int_{V}\Gamma_{0}(x,x^{\prime})\Delta\chi(x^{\prime})\psi(x^{\prime}).

(G.50)

The total variation can be written as:

\displaystyle\delta f

\displaystyle=2\operatorname{Re}\int_{V}\int_{V}\psi(x^{\prime})^{\rm T}\Delta\chi(x^{\prime})\Gamma_{0}^{\rm T}(x,x^{\prime})\frac{\partial f}{\partial\psi(x)}.

(G.51)

Using reciprocity relations,
\Gamma_{0}^{\rm T}(x,x^{\prime})=Q^{\rm T}\Gamma_{0}(x^{\prime},x)Q
, where
Q=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{I}&0\\
0&-\mathbb{I}\end{pmatrix}
is the parity operator. Then by rearranging, the variation in the figure of merit is given by

\displaystyle\delta f

\displaystyle=2\operatorname{Re}\int_{V}\psi(x^{\prime})^{\rm T}\Delta\chi(x^{\prime})Q\int_{V}\Gamma(x^{\prime},x)Q\frac{\partial f}{\partial\psi(x)}.

(G.52)

Now one can define the adjoint field
\psi_{\rm adj}(x^{\prime})=\int_{V}\Gamma(x^{\prime},x)Q\frac{\partial f}{\partial\psi(x)}
, which is essentially fields resulting from the six-vector current sources, the so-called adjoint sources,
\phi_{\rm adj}=Q\frac{\partial f}{\partial\psi}
.
In the case of absorption cross-section
\sigma_{\rm abs}
, the adjoint sources are given by

\phi_{\rm adj}=\frac{1}{A}\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\operatorname{Im}\chi}{|\chi|^{2}}Q\psi^{\star},

(G.53)

and so the variation in
\sigma_{\rm abs}
is

\displaystyle\delta\sigma_{\rm abs}=2\operatorname{Re}\int_{V}\psi(x^{\prime})^{\rm T}\Delta\chi(x^{\prime})Q\psi_{\rm adj}(x^{\prime}).

(G.54)

Hence the fields
\psi
from the prescribed structure with direct incidence plus the adjoint fields
\psi_{\rm adj}
provide the gradients with respect to any number of design variables. Numerically, in each iteration of the topology optimizations, one direct simulation to compute
\psi
and another simulation with
\phi_{\rm adj}
as sources to compute
\psi_{\rm adj}
are need.

The simulations are performed with a finite-difference time-domain
[
122
]
open-source solver
[
331
]
. In all design figures below, periodic conditions are imposed in the horizontal direction, and light is incident from below and propagating upward. For all sets of hyper-parameters, including material permittivities and thicknesses, we test at least 10 initial starting points, and run simulations with resolutions up to 110 grids per wavelength. Almost all optimizations converge within 700 iterations, and we show in Fig.
G.2
the evolution of
\sigma_{\rm abs}
in
1.2\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro m}
-thick SiC absorber optimization.

Figure G.2:
Percent absorption as a function the number of iterations for the best design optimization of
1.2\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro m}
-thick SiC absorber.

G.7 Optimized designs

G.7.1
Different thicknesses of SiC absorbers at
11\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro m}
wavelength

As an example, we investigated absorber inverse designs with SiC at
11\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro m}
wavelength and a range of thicknesses. The resolution is
0.1\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro m}
, and unit cell period is
1.1\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro m}
. Their percent absorption and designs are presented in Table
G.3
.

G.7.2
Minimum thicknesses of 70% absorbers for different materials

Thinnest perfect absorbers are designed for different types of materials, such as metals, doped semiconductors and polar dielectrics. In Table
G.4
, we demonstrate designs of six representative materials at different wavelengths where 70% absorption is achieved with minimum thicknesses of the metasurfaces.

G.8 Deriving previous bounds from general bound formalism

Different derivations of upper bounds can be formulated as optimization problems with same objective functions but different constraints. In this section, we showed that how the analytical bound, developed in Chapter
2
, can incoorperate previous bounds by either relaxing the energy equality constraint, or taking the result of the general bound in certain limit.

Upper bounds as optimization problems with the same objective function but different constraints. General bound uses an equality energy constraint, while the other two relax it to inequality constraints (or even unconstrained), resulting looser bounds.

Optimum of different upper-bound formulations presented in Table

. Optimal dual variable of the general bound is determined by Eq. (

).

Channel and lossy-material bounds

Table
G.1
compares general bound with lossy-material bound (material bound) and channel bound.
General bound purposed in Chapter
2
utilizes the
equality
energy conservation constraint:
P_{\rm scat}+P_{\rm abs}=P_{\rm ext}
. Throwing away either
P_{\rm scat}
or
P_{\rm abs}
gives the
inequality
energy conservation constraint used in previous material bound
[
61
]
or channel bound
[
56
]
. In both formalisms, the disregarded term itself is treated by an unconstrained optimization.

All optimization problems in Table
G.1
have strong duality, thus their optimums can be analytically determined by the optimal of their dual functions, given in Table
G.2
(with prefactor
\omega/2
suppressed in every expression). Results for material bound appears in
[
61
]
. Results for channel bound appears in
[
56
]
. Moreover, expanding channel bound into VSWs for a spherical scatterer gives the expressions in
[
51
,
52
,
53
,
54
]
(after adding back prefactor
\omega/2
):
P^{\rm max}_{\rm scat}=P^{\rm max}_{\rm ext}=4P^{\rm max}_{\rm abs}=\frac{|E_{0}|^{2}}{k^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\pi(2n+1)
, where
E_{0}
is the plane wave amplitude,
k
is the amplitude of the wave vector, and
n
is total angular momentum.

\mathbb{T}
-operator bound

As discussed in Section
G
, our bound is tighter than
\mathbb{T}
-operator bound
[
332
]
for maximum absorption from a thermal incident field. Though using different approaches, the general bound can reproduce the same result as in
\mathbb{T}
-operator bound by relaxing the energy constraint to
P_{\rm abs}\leq P_{\rm ext}
and replacing objective function
P_{\rm abs}
with
P_{\rm ext}-P_{\rm scat}
:

\displaystyle\text{maximize}

\displaystyle P_{\rm ext}-P_{\rm scat}

(G.55)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle P_{\rm abs}\leq P_{\rm ext}.

Similar to Section
G
, we solve Eq. (
G.55
) by its dual function:

\displaystyle g(\lambda)=\frac{(\lambda+1)^{2}}{4}e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\left[\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}+\lambda\operatorname{Im}\xi\mathbb{I}\right]^{-1}e_{\rm inc},

(G.56)

where
\lambda
is the notation used in
[
118
]
to denote dual variable for an inequality constraint. The range for
\lambda
is
[0,+\infty)
. When
\lambda=0
, the inverse operator in Eq. (
G.56
) is ill-defined and we replace it with pseudo inverse if
e_{\rm inc}\in\text{Range}\{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}\}
, otherwise
g(0)\rightarrow-\infty

Following assumptions made in
\mathbb{T}
-operator bound, we assume far-field thermal incidence and nonmagnetic material, where
e_{\rm inc}
and
\Gamma_{0}
is replaced by
e_{\rm inc}
and
\mathbb{G}_{0}
. As discussed in Section
G
, thermal incident field can be expanded by a set of uncorrelated orthogonal fields. We choose it to be
\{v_{i}\}
, the eigenvectors of
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}
, with expansion coefficients given by
|e_{i}|^{2}=\frac{4}{\pi\omega}\Theta(T)
and
\Theta(T)=\hbar\omega/(e^{\hbar\omega/k_{B}T}-1)
is the Planck energy of a harmonic oscillator at temperature
T
.

Maximizing
g(\lambda)
gives the expression for optimal absorption of thermal incident fields in
[
332
]
:

P_{\text{abs}}\leq\frac{2}{\pi}\Theta(T)\sum_{i}\begin{cases}\frac{\rho_{i}}{\operatorname{Im}{\xi}}&\text{for }2\rho_{i}\leq\operatorname{Im}{\xi}\\
\frac{1}{4}&\text{for }2\rho_{i}\geq\operatorname{Im}{\xi},\end{cases}

(G.57)

where two cases correspond to optimal dual variable taking the value of either
\nu_{1}\in(0,+\infty)
or
\nu_{0}=0
. Such a bound is looser than the general bound presented in Section
G
, as a result of its inequality energy constraint in Eq. (
G.55
), rather than the equality energy constraint.

Patterned thin film bound

It is predicted that within a vacuum background, a patterned thin film with thickness much smaller than the incident wavelength has a maximum absorption of 50%
[
48
]
. To validate this, we take the limit
k_{z}h\rightarrow 0
in Eq. (
G.43
) and obtain:

\sigma_{\rm abs}/A\leq\frac{2(\operatorname{Im}{\xi})\rho_{+,p^{\prime}}}{(\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\rho_{+,p^{\prime}})^{2}}.

(G.58)

Because a thin film only has dipole radiation that is symmetric respect to the
z=0
plane, only mode with index
s=+
survived in Eq. (
G.58
).

When
\operatorname{Im}\xi=\rho_{+,M}
, the absorption rate
\sigma_{\rm abs}/A
in Eq. (
G.58
) reaches its maximum of 50%, agreeing with the prediction made in
[
48
]
. The advantage of our formalism is that we can also predict the minimum thickness for the patterned thin film to reach
50\%
absorption:
h_{\rm min}=2\operatorname{Im}\xi/k
, as solved from the optimal condition
\operatorname{Im}\xi=\rho_{+,M}
.

G.9 Underestimation of the channel bounds from cutoff channels

The channel bounds shown in Table
G.2
are in fact infinite for a plane wave incident. Physically, this is due to the negligible radiative loss in high-order VSW channels, corresponding to the eigenvectors of
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}
with near-zero eigenvalues. To regularize such divergence, one needs to truncate its radiation channels to a finite number based on certain threshold. Such an empirical truncation is certainly a disadvantage of the channel bound, moreover, as we will show below, it also introduces unwanted underestimation of the channel bound itself.

As an example, Fig.
G.3
shows channel bounds for per-channel extinction
\sigma_{\text{ext},n}
within a bounding volume of radius
R=10\lambda
. The material is Ag and incident wavelength
\lambda=360
nm. Also shown in the same figure are the general bound and spherical scattering. As expected, the channel bound diverges at high-order radiative channels, and is regularized by a 1% cutoff line, which excludes channels for which the sphere scattering contributions are less than 1% of the channel bound.

Compared with the general bound, we see that the potential contribution of those excluded channels (red shaded region), are ignored by the 1% threshold. Such an underestimation results in a seemly tighter bound in Fig.
2.2
in Chapter
2
at large radius limit. Of course, the 1% threshold is empirical. One could reduce the threshold to eliminate the unwanted underestimation, but that usually results in an overall overestimation of the channel bound since more channels are now included without the inhibition of material loss. We found 1% is a good empirical threshold for estimating the channel bound.

Figure G.3:
Per-channel extinction
\sigma_{\text{ext},n}
for material Ag in a spherical bounding volume with radius
R=10\lambda
at wavelength
\lambda=360
nm. Compared to the general bound, the 1% cutoff threshold excludes channels whose potential contributions are marked by the red shaded region, resulting in an underestimated channel bound.

G.10 The imaginary part of the Green’s function operator for a sphere

The expressions of
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}
is given in
[
249
]
, whose imaginary part is Hermitian and can be decomposed as:

\operatorname{Im}{\mathbb{G}_{0}}=\frac{1}{2i}(\mathbb{G}_{0}-\mathbb{G}^{\rm EE\dagger}_{0})=\sum_{n,m,j}v_{n,m,j}v_{n,m,j}^{\dagger},

(G.59)

where
n=1,2,...
,
m=-n,...,n
,
j=1,2
represents two polarizations.
v_{n,m,j}
are reguarized VSWs whose definition can be found in Ref.
[
249
]
:

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{n,m,1}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=k^{\frac{3}{2}}Rg\bm{M}_{n,m}(kr,\theta,\phi)

(G.60)

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{n,m,2}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=k^{\frac{3}{2}}Rg\bm{N}_{n,m}(kr,\theta,\phi).

(G.61)

The inner product of
v_{n,m,j}
with itself gives the eigenvalue of
\operatorname{Im}{\mathbb{G}_{0}}
:

\displaystyle\rho_{n,m,j}

\displaystyle=v_{n,m,j}^{\dagger}v_{n,m,j}

(G.62)

\displaystyle=\int_{V}\bm{v}_{n,m,j}^{*}(\bm{x})\cdot\bm{v}_{n,m,j}(\bm{x})\text{d}V.

(G.63)

Integrating over angular coordinates gives the expression:

\displaystyle\rho_{n,m,1}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{kR}x^{2}j_{n}^{2}(x)\text{d}x

(G.64)

\displaystyle\rho_{n,m,2}

\displaystyle=n(n+1)\int_{0}^{kR}j_{n}^{2}(x)\text{d}x+\int_{0}^{kR}[xj_{n}(x)]^{\prime 2}\text{d}x,

(G.65)

which can be computationally evaluated or even reduced to simpler analytical forms
[
333
]
.

G.11 The imaginary part of the Green’s function operator for a film

As in Refs.
[
249
,
334
]
,
\operatorname{Im}{\mathbb{G}_{0}}
in Cartesian coordinate can be decomposed into a complete set of plane waves:

\operatorname{Im}{\mathbb{G}_{0}}=\sum_{s,p}\int_{k_{\parallel}\leq k}\bm{v}_{s,p}(\bm{k_{\parallel}})\bm{v}^{\dagger}_{s,p}(\bm{k_{\parallel}})\frac{\text{d}\bm{k_{\parallel}}}{(2\pi)^{2}}.

(G.66)

Index
s=\{-1,+1\}
represents odd and even parity, index
p=M,N
represents different polarization,
\bm{k_{\parallel}}
are in-plane wave vector whose integration only runs through propergating modes.
Real-space expressions of
v_{s,p}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})
are:

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{+,M}(\bm{k_{\parallel}},\bm{x})

\displaystyle=ik\frac{e^{i\bm{k}_{\parallel}\cdot\bm{r}_{\parallel}}}{\sqrt{2k_{z}}k_{\parallel}}(k_{y}\bm{\hat{e}}_{x}-k_{x}\bm{\hat{e}}_{y})\cos(k_{z}z)

(G.67)

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{-,M}(\bm{k_{\parallel}},\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-ik\frac{e^{i\bm{k}_{\parallel}\cdot\bm{r}_{\parallel}}}{\sqrt{2k_{z}}k_{\parallel}}(k_{y}\bm{\hat{e}}_{x}-k_{x}\bm{\hat{e}}_{y})\sin(k_{z}z)

(G.68)

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{+,N}(\bm{k_{\parallel}},\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{i\bm{k}_{\parallel}\cdot\bm{r}_{\parallel}}}{\sqrt{2k_{z}}}\left[k_{\parallel}\cos(k_{z}z)\bm{\hat{z}}-ik_{z}\sin(k_{z}z)\bm{\hat{k}_{\parallel}}\right]

(G.69)

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{-,N}(\bm{k_{\parallel}},\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{i\bm{k}_{\parallel}\cdot\bm{r}_{\parallel}}}{\sqrt{2k_{z}}}\left[k_{\parallel}\sin(k_{z}z)\bm{\hat{z}}+ik_{z}\cos(k_{z}z)\bm{\hat{k}_{\parallel}}\right].

(G.70)

Inner products of
v_{s,p}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})
in a thin film (thickness
h
, centered at
z=0
) is
[
332
]
:

\displaystyle v_{s,p}^{\dagger}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})v_{s^{\prime},p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{V}\bm{v}_{s,p}^{*}(\bm{k}_{\parallel},\bm{x})\cdot\bm{v}_{s^{\prime},p^{\prime}}(\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime},\bm{x})\text{d}V

(G.71)

\displaystyle=\rho_{s,p}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\bm{k}_{\parallel}-\bm{k}_{\parallel}^{\prime})\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}\delta_{p,p^{\prime}},

(G.72)

where the eigenvalues are:

\displaystyle\rho_{\pm,M}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}h}{4k_{z}}(1\pm\frac{\sin(k_{z}h)}{k_{z}h})

(G.73)

\displaystyle\rho_{\pm,N}(\bm{k}_{\parallel})

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}h}{4k_{z}}(1\pm\frac{\sin(k_{z}h)}{k_{z}h})\mp\frac{\sin(k_{z}h)}{2}.

(G.74)

G.12 Upper bounds at different generality

For generality, we first express the bounds with six-vector notations for the incident field
\psi_{\rm inc}
, Green’s function
\Gamma_{0}
, and implicitly for
\operatorname{Im}\xi
which is now a tensor operator for arbitrary material susceptibility.

1.

Most general form (include non-local, magnetic, inhomogeneous materials, any incident field, any geometry of the scatterer):

\displaystyle P_{\rm ext}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\psi_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\left(\operatorname{Im}\xi+\operatorname{Im}\Gamma_{0}\right)^{-1}\psi_{\rm inc}

(G.75)

\displaystyle P_{\rm abs}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\psi_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}[(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}\xi+\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\Gamma_{0}]^{-1}\psi_{\rm inc}

(G.76)

\displaystyle P_{\rm scat}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\psi_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}[\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\xi+(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}\Gamma_{0}]^{-1}\psi_{\rm inc},

(G.77)

where
\operatorname{Im}\xi
and
\operatorname{Im}\Gamma_{0}
are matrices that depends on the exact shape and material compositions of the scatterer.

2.

Scalar material (electric or magnetic scalar material, any incident field, any geometry of the homogeneous scatterer):

\displaystyle P_{\rm ext}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}{\xi}}\Big[\psi_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\psi_{\rm inc}-\psi_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\mathbb{V}(\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}\mathbb{V})^{-1}\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}\psi_{\rm inc}\Big]

(G.78)

\displaystyle P_{\rm abs}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\frac{\nu^{*}}{\nu^{*}-1}\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}{\xi}}\Big\{\frac{1}{\nu^{*}}\psi_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\psi_{\rm inc}

\displaystyle-\psi_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\mathbb{V}[(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\nu^{*}\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}\mathbb{V}]^{-1}\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}\psi_{\rm inc}\Big\}

(G.79)

\displaystyle P_{\rm scat}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\frac{\nu^{*}-1}{\nu^{*}}\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}{\xi}}\Big\{\frac{1}{\nu^{*}-1}\psi_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\psi_{\rm inc}

\displaystyle-\psi_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\mathbb{V}[\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+(\nu^{*}-1)\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}\mathbb{V}]^{-1}\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}\psi_{\rm inc}\Big\},

(G.80)

where
\operatorname{Im}\xi
is a scalar represents either the isotropic electric or magnetic susceptibility and we write the eigendecomposition of
\operatorname{Im}\Gamma_{0}
as
\operatorname{Im}\Gamma_{0}=\mathbb{V}\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}
.

3.

Isotropic
electric
material (electric scalar material, any incident field, any geometry): same form as Eqs. (
G.78
)–(
G.80
) with
\psi_{\rm inc}
replaced by
e_{\rm inc}
, and
\Gamma_{0}
replaced by
\mathbb{G}_{0}
. Eigenbasis
\mathbb{V}
is now defined by the eigendecomposition:
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}=\mathbb{V}\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}
, with
v_{i}
being the i-th column of
\mathbb{V}
.

(a)

For far field scattering, where the incident electric field
e_{\rm inc}
is characterized by the property
e_{\rm inc}\in\text{Range}\{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}\}
, bounds in Eqs. (
G.78
)–(
G.80
) can be dramatically simplified:

\displaystyle P_{\rm ext}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}(\operatorname{Im}\xi+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0})^{-1}e_{\rm inc}

(G.81)

\displaystyle P_{\rm abs}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}[(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}\xi+\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}]^{-1}e_{\rm inc}

(G.82)

\displaystyle P_{\rm scat}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}[\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\xi+(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}]^{-1}e_{\rm inc}.

(G.83)

•

Plane wave incidence (applies to both finite and extended scatterers) with
e_{\rm inc}=\sum_{i}e_{i}v_{i}
. Explicitly written out contributions from different channels:

\displaystyle P_{\rm ext}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\sum_{i}|e_{i}|^{2}\frac{\rho_{i}}{\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\rho_{i}}

(G.84)

\displaystyle P_{\rm abs}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\sum_{i}|e_{i}|^{2}\frac{\rho_{i}}{(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\nu^{*}\rho_{i}}

(G.85)

\displaystyle P_{\rm scat}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\sum_{i}|e_{i}|^{2}\frac{\rho_{i}}{\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+(\nu^{*}-1)\rho_{i}},

(G.86)

where
\rho_{i}=v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}
is analytically known for highly symmetric bounding volumes.

•

VSW incidence (applies to finite scatterers). Now the incident field is one specific VSW:
e_{\rm inc}=e_{i}v_{i}
, under which:

\displaystyle P_{\rm ext}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{|e_{i}|^{2}}{\operatorname{Im}\xi+\rho_{i}}

(G.87)

\displaystyle P_{\rm abs}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\frac{|e_{i}|^{2}}{(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}\xi+\nu^{*}\rho_{i}}=\begin{cases}\frac{\operatorname{Im}\xi}{(\operatorname{Im}\xi+\rho_{i})^{2}}|e_{i}|^{2}&\text{if\ \ }\rho_{i}\leq\operatorname{Im}\xi\\
\frac{1}{4\rho_{i}}|e_{i}|^{2}&\rm{else}\end{cases}

(G.88)

\displaystyle P_{\rm scat}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4}\frac{|e_{i}|^{2}}{\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\xi+(\nu^{*}-1)\rho_{i}}=\begin{cases}\frac{\rho_{i}}{(\operatorname{Im}\xi+\rho_{\rm max})^{2}}|e_{i}|^{2}&\hskip-55.0pt\text{if\ \ }\rho_{i}\geq\frac{\rho_{\rm max}\operatorname{Im}\xi}{2\operatorname{Im}\xi+\rho_{\rm max}}\\
\frac{1}{4}\frac{\rho_{\rm max}^{2}}{\operatorname{Im}\xi(\rho_{\rm max}+\operatorname{Im}\xi)(\rho_{\rm max}-\rho_{i})}|e_{i}|^{2}&\rm{else},\end{cases}

(G.89)

where the choice of
\nu^{*}
is simple enough that we can write out explicit two possible solutions of
P_{\rm abs}
and
P_{\rm sca}
. We denote the maximum in
\{\rho_{i}\}
as
\rho_{\rm max}
.

(b)

Incident field in near-field scattering is not necessarily in the range of
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}
as evanescent waves may contribute (for an extended scatter). Expression for its bound takes the most general form as Eqs. (
G.78
)–(
G.80
) with
\psi_{\rm inc}
replaced by
e_{\rm inc}
, and
\Gamma_{0}
replaced by
\mathbb{G}_{0}
.
For arbitrary dipole sources
p
, the incident field can be written as
\bm{e}_{\rm inc}=\mathbb{G}_{0,p\rightarrow V}p
where
\mathbb{G}_{0,p\rightarrow V}
is an integral Green’s function mapped from the region of dipole source
p
to the scatterer
V
. Taking the singular vector decomposition of
\mathbb{G}_{0,p\rightarrow V}=\mathbb{U}\mathbb{W}^{\dagger}
, bounds for near field scattering can be written as:

\displaystyle P_{\rm ext}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}\xi}p^{\dagger}\mathbb{W}[\mathbb{U}^{\dagger}\mathbb{U}-\mathbb{U}^{\dagger}\mathbb{V}(\operatorname{Im}\xi+\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}\mathbb{V})^{-1}\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}\mathbb{U}]\mathbb{W}^{\dagger}p

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}\xi}\sum_{i}|p^{\dagger}w_{i}|^{2}\left(u_{i}^{\dagger}u_{i}-\frac{|u_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}|^{2}}{\operatorname{Im}\xi+v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}}\right)

(G.90)

\displaystyle P_{\rm abs}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4(\nu^{*}-1)}\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}\xi}p^{\dagger}\mathbb{W}\{\mathbb{U}^{\dagger}\mathbb{U}-\mathbb{U}^{\dagger}\mathbb{V}[(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}\xi/\nu^{*}+\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}\mathbb{V}]^{-1}\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}\mathbb{U}\}\mathbb{W}^{\dagger}p

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4(\nu^{*}-1)}\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}\xi}\sum_{i}|p^{\dagger}w_{i}|^{2}\left(u_{i}^{\dagger}u_{i}-\frac{|u_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}|^{2}}{(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}\xi/\nu^{*}+v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}}\right)

(G.91)

\displaystyle P_{\rm scat}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*}}{4}\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}\xi}p^{\dagger}\mathbb{W}\{\mathbb{U}^{\dagger}\mathbb{U}-\mathbb{U}^{\dagger}\mathbb{V}[\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\xi/(\nu^{*}-1)+\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}\mathbb{V}]^{-1}\mathbb{V}^{\dagger}\mathbb{U}\}\mathbb{W}^{\dagger}p

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega}{2}\frac{\nu^{*}}{4}\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}\xi}\sum_{i}|p^{\dagger}w_{i}|^{2}\left(u_{i}^{\dagger}u_{i}-\frac{|u_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}|^{2}}{\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\xi/(\nu^{*}-1)+v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}}\right).

(G.92)

G.13 Bound for local density of states (LDOS)

In this section, we first derive general bounds of LDOS quantities for arbitrary materials in six-vector notations (defined in Section
G
), then narrow the scope down to non-magnetic materials to simplify the expressions of the bounds.

General bounds for LDOS

We start with the expressions of total, non-radiative, radiative electric LDOS in six-vector volume-integral form
[
61
]
:

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm tot}

\displaystyle=\rho_{0}+\frac{1}{\pi\omega}\sum_{j}\operatorname{Im}\left(\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc,j}^{\dagger}\phi_{j}\right)

(G.93)

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm nr}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi\omega}\sum_{j}\phi_{j}^{\dagger}(\operatorname{Im}\xi)\phi_{j}

(G.94)

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm rad}

\displaystyle=\rho_{0}+\frac{1}{\pi\omega}\sum_{j}\left[\operatorname{Im}\left(\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc,j}^{\dagger}\phi_{j}\right)-\phi_{j}^{\dagger}(\operatorname{Im}\xi)\phi_{j}\right],

(G.95)

where
\rho_{0}
is the electric LDOS of the background material, and takes the value of
\frac{\omega^{2}}{2\pi^{2}c^{3}}
for a scatterer in the vacuum
[
128
]
.
Summation
j=1,2,3
denotes power quantities from three orthogonally polarized unit dipoles. Incident field from dipole
j
is denoted by
\psi_{\rm inc,j}=(e_{\rm inc,j},h_{\rm inc,j})
. Here we use lowercase notations for both electric and magnetic fields to emphasis their vector nature, as opposed to capitalized characters that are usually reserved for operators and matrices. Such incident field excites polarization field
\phi_{j}
in the scatterer. Complex conjugate of
\psi_{\rm inc,j}
(with a minus sign in front of magnetic fields) is denoted by
\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc,j}=(e^{*}_{\rm inc,j},-h^{*}_{\rm inc,j})
.

Because three dipoles are uncorrelated, we can first solve the bound for one unit dipole. For simplicity, we omit its index
j
and write its incident field as
\psi_{\rm inc}
, which excites polarization field
\phi
in the body. For this dipole, its non-radiative LDOS can be bounded by maximum absorption in Eq. (
G.18
) by identifying the objective function as
\phi^{\dagger}(\operatorname{Im}\xi)\phi
. Bounds on total and radiative LDOS are less straightforward and are discussed below.

Objective function for total LDOS is
\operatorname{Im}\left(\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\phi\right)
with energy conservation constraint
\phi^{\dagger}\left(\operatorname{Im}\xi+\operatorname{Im}\Gamma_{0}\right)\phi=\operatorname{Im}\left(\psi_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\phi\right)
. This six-vector form is generalized from Eq. (
G.1
) with
\mathbb{A}=0
and
\beta=\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}
. Its maximum is given by Eq. (
G.7
):

\displaystyle\max_{\phi}\left\{\operatorname{Im}\left(\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\phi\right)\right\}=\frac{1}{4\nu^{*}}(\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}+\nu^{*}\psi_{\rm inc})^{\dagger}\left(\operatorname{Im}\xi+\operatorname{Im}\Gamma_{0}\right)^{-1}(\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}+\nu^{*}\psi_{\rm inc}),

(G.96)

where the optimal dual variable
\nu^{*}
is always chosen at
\nu_{1}>\nu_{0}=0
similar to Eq. (
G.10
):

\nu^{*}=\nu_{1}=\left[\frac{\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\left(\operatorname{Im}\xi+\operatorname{Im}\Gamma_{0}\right)^{-1}\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}}{\psi_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\left(\operatorname{Im}\xi+\operatorname{Im}\Gamma_{0}\right)^{-1}\psi_{\rm inc}}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(G.97)

For non-magnetic scatterer, the above expression can be significantly simplified. No magnetic current can be excited in the non-magnetic scatterer such that
\bm{M}(\bm{x})=0
. Examining the object function
\operatorname{Im}\left(\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\phi\right)
, we can find that it is equivalent to set
h_{\rm inc}=0
. Equation (
G.97
) gives
\nu^{*}=1
and the maximum objective function for non-magnetic scatterer can be simplified to:

\displaystyle\max_{\phi}\left\{\operatorname{Im}\left(\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\phi\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left[\operatorname{Re}e_{\rm inc}\right]^{\dagger}\left(\operatorname{Im}\xi+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}\right)^{-1}\left[\operatorname{Re}e_{\rm inc}\right]

(G.98)

\displaystyle\leq e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\left(\operatorname{Im}\xi+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}\right)^{-1}e_{\rm inc}

(G.99)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\omega}P^{\rm max}_{\rm ext}.

(G.100)

where in the last two lines, we relax the bound to the maximum-extinction bound given in Eq. (
G.14
) with the same assumption of non-magnetic scatterer.

Objective function for radiative LDOS defined in Eq. (
G.95
) can be chosen as
\operatorname{Im}\left(\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\phi\right)-\phi^{\dagger}(\operatorname{Im}\xi)\phi
. Thus,
\mathbb{A}=-\operatorname{Im}\xi
,
\beta=\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}
. Maximal objective function given be Eq. (
G.7
) can be written as:

\displaystyle\max_{\phi}

\displaystyle\left\{\operatorname{Im}\left(\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\phi\right)-\phi^{\dagger}(\operatorname{Im}\xi)\phi\right\}=

(G.101)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}(\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}+\nu^{*}\psi_{\rm inc})^{\dagger}\left[(\nu^{*}+1)\operatorname{Im}\xi+\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\Gamma_{0}\right]^{-1}(\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}+\nu^{*}\psi_{\rm inc})

with optimal dual variable
\nu^{*}
given by Eq. (
G.11
). For non-magnetic scatterer (effectively
h_{\rm inc}=0
in Eq. (
G.101
)), bound in Eq. (
G.101
) reduces to:

\displaystyle\max_{\phi}

\displaystyle\left\{\operatorname{Im}\left(\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\phi\right)-\phi^{\dagger}(\operatorname{Im}\xi)\phi\right\}

(G.102)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(\tilde{e}_{\rm inc}+\nu^{*}e_{\rm inc})^{\dagger}\left[(\nu^{*}+1)\operatorname{Im}\xi+\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}\right]^{-1}(\tilde{e}_{\rm inc}+\nu^{*}e_{\rm inc}).

Radiative LDOS bound in Eq. (
G.102
) can be relaxed to scattering bound in Eq. (
G.21
) by observing that the dual function of the former,
g_{1}(\nu)
, is always greater than or equal to the latter (after suppressing its
\frac{\omega}{2}
factor),
g_{2}(\nu)
, for any
\nu\geq\nu_{0}
:

\displaystyle g_{1}(\nu)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}(\tilde{e}_{\rm inc}+\nu e_{\rm inc})^{\dagger}\left[(\nu+1)\operatorname{Im}\xi+\nu\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}\right]^{-1}(\tilde{e}_{\rm inc}+\nu e_{\rm inc})

(G.103)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{(1+\nu)^{2}}{4}e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\left[(\nu+1)\operatorname{Im}\xi+\nu\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}\right]^{-1}e_{\rm inc}=g_{2}(\nu).

(G.104)

The last inequality can be proved by performing Cholesky decomposition on the Hermitian matrix
\left[(\nu+1)\operatorname{Im}\xi+\nu\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}\right]^{-1}=\mathbb{L}^{\dagger}\mathbb{L}
and using Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to relax the cross term:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\{e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\mathbb{L}^{\dagger}\mathbb{L}\bm{e}^{*}_{\rm inc}\}

\displaystyle\leq\left|e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\mathbb{L}^{\dagger}\mathbb{L}\bm{e}^{*}_{\rm inc}\right|

(G.105)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathbb{L}e_{\rm inc}\right\|\cdot\left\|\mathbb{L}\bm{e}^{*}_{\rm inc}\right\|=\left\|\mathbb{L}e_{\rm inc}\right\|^{2}=e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\mathbb{L}^{\dagger}\mathbb{L}e_{\rm inc}.

It follows from Eq. (
G.104
) that the maximum of
g_{1}(\nu)
is greater than the maximum of
g_{2}(\nu)
. The optimum of a primal function is given by the negative of the maximum of a dual function, so the optimal objective function considered here is smaller than the optimal scattering bound in Eq. (
G.21
), and equivalently Eq. (
G.20
):

\max_{\phi}\left\{\operatorname{Im}\left(\tilde{\psi}_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\phi\right)-\phi^{\dagger}(\operatorname{Im}\xi)\phi\right\}\leq\frac{2}{\omega}P_{\rm sca}^{\rm max}.

(G.106)

To summarize, we derive general LDOS bounds for any material. For non-magnetic material specifically, LDOS can be directly bounded by maximum power response in Eqs. (
G.14
), (
G.18
), and (
G.20
):

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm tot}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\pi\omega^{2}}\sum_{j}P^{\rm max}_{\text{ext},j}+\rho_{0}

(G.107)

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm nr}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\pi\omega^{2}}\sum_{j}P^{\rm max}_{\text{abs},j}

(G.108)

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm rad}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\pi\omega^{2}}\sum_{j}P^{\rm max}_{\text{sca},j}+\rho_{0},

(G.109)

where
j=1,2,3
denotes the summation of maximum power quantities from three orthogonally polarized unit dipoles.

LDOS bounds for a finite non-magnetic scatterer

In the following, we assume the scatterer is non-magnetic and finite, embedded in the vacuum. The non-magnetic nature of the scatterer allows us to use Eqs. (
G.107
)–(
G.109
) to decompose LDOS bounds to previous power bounds for three orthogonally polarized unit dipoles. In Eqs. (
G.90
)–(
G.92
), we presented power bounds for arbitrary dipole distributions
\bm{p}(\bm{x})
. Here, we start with a point dipole oriented along
\bm{\hat{e}}_{j}
at origin
\bm{p}(\bm{x})=\bm{p}_{j}(\bm{x})=p_{0}\delta(\bm{x})\bm{\hat{e}}_{j}
with
p_{0}=1
, and later sum up the contributions from three orthogonal polarizations.
We also assume the scatterer is finite, thus can be enclosed by a spherical shell (see Fig.
G.4
inset).
A shell-like bounding volume has spherical symmetry, so
v_{i}
and
w_{i}
in Eqs. (
G.90
)–(
G.92
) are regular VSWs:

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{mn1}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\bm{w}_{mn1}(\bm{x})=k^{\frac{3}{2}}Rg\bm{M}_{mn}(kr,\theta,\phi)

(G.110)

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{mn2}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\bm{w}_{mn2}(\bm{x})=k^{\frac{3}{2}}Rg\bm{N}_{mn}(kr,\theta,\phi),

(G.111)

u_{i}
are outgoing VSWs:

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{mn1}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=k^{\frac{3}{2}}\bm{M}_{mn}(kr,\theta,\phi)

(G.112)

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{mn2}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=k^{\frac{3}{2}}\bm{N}_{mn}(kr,\theta,\phi).

(G.113)

Power bounds in Eqs. (
G.90
)–(
G.92
) require us to evaluate four overlap integrals:
p_{j}^{\dagger}w_{i},u_{i}^{\dagger}u_{i},u_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i},v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}
.
We first evaluate overlap integral between the point dipole and regular VSWs in the source volume
V_{s}
:

\displaystyle p_{j}^{\dagger}w_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{V_{s}}\bm{p}_{j}^{*}(\bm{x})\cdot\bm{w}_{i}(\bm{x})\text{d}\bm{x}

(G.114)

\displaystyle=p_{0}\bm{\hat{e}}_{j}\cdot\bm{w}_{i}(\bm{x}=0)

(G.115)

\displaystyle=k^{\frac{3}{2}}p_{0}\begin{cases}\bm{\hat{e}}_{j}\cdot Rg\bm{N}_{m,1}(0,\theta,\phi)&\text{if }j=2,n=1\\
0&\text{else,}\end{cases}

(G.116)

where we used the fact that only
Rg\bm{N}_{m,1}
has nonzero value at the origin. Exact value of the dot product
\bm{e}_{j}\cdot Rg\bm{N}_{m,1}(0,\theta,\phi)
depends on the orientation of the dipole:

\bm{\hat{e}}_{j}\cdot Rg\bm{N}_{m,1}(0,\theta,\phi)=\begin{cases}\pm\frac{1}{2\sqrt{3\pi}}\delta_{m,\pm 1}&\text{if\ }\bm{\hat{e}}_{j}=\bm{\hat{x}}\\
\frac{1}{2i\sqrt{3\pi}}\delta_{m,\pm 1}&\text{if\ }\bm{\hat{e}}_{j}=\bm{\hat{y}}\\
-\frac{1}{\sqrt{6\pi}}\delta_{m,0}&\text{if\ }\bm{\hat{e}}_{j}=\bm{\hat{z}}.\end{cases}

(G.117)

Later for LDOS, we will need to evaluate averaged power from three randomly oriented dipoles, which is related to the quantity:

\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j}|p_{j}^{\dagger}w_{i}|^{2}=k^{3}\frac{p_{0}^{3}}{18\pi}\delta_{n,1}\delta_{j,2},

(G.118)

where
\bm{\hat{e}}_{j}
runs through directions
\bm{\hat{x}}
,
\bm{\hat{y}}
, and
\bm{\hat{z}}
.
We now evaluate overlap integrals between different VSWs within the bounding volume
V
:

\displaystyle v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{V}\bm{v}_{mnj}^{*}(\bm{x})\cdot\bm{v}_{mnj}(\bm{x})\text{d}\bm{x}=I_{j}\left(j_{n}^{(1)}(x),j_{n}^{(1)}(x)\right)

(G.119)

\displaystyle u_{i}^{\dagger}u_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{V}\bm{u}_{mnj}^{*}(\bm{x})\cdot\bm{u}_{mnj}(\bm{x})\text{d}\bm{x}=I_{j}\left(h_{n}^{(1)*}(x),h_{n}^{(1)}(x)\right)

(G.120)

\displaystyle u_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{V}\bm{u}_{mnj}^{*}(\bm{x})\cdot\bm{v}_{mnj}(\bm{x})\text{d}\bm{x}=I_{j}\left(h_{n}^{(1)*}(x),j_{n}^{(1)}(x)\right),

(G.121)

where we defined function:

I_{j}\left(z_{n}^{(1)}(x),z_{n}^{(2)}(x)\right)=\begin{cases}\int_{kR_{1}}^{kR_{2}}x^{2}z_{n}^{(1)}(x)z_{n}^{(2)}(x)\text{d}x&\text{if }j=1\\
n(n+1)\int_{kR_{1}}^{kR_{2}}z_{n}^{(1)}(x)z_{n}^{(2)}(x)\text{d}x\\
\quad+\int_{kR_{1}}^{kR_{2}}[xz_{n}^{(1)}(x)]^{\prime}[xz_{n}^{(2)}(x)]^{\prime}\text{d}x&\text{if }j=2.\end{cases}

(G.122)

Bound for total extinction from three randomly oriented dipoles is bounded by:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j}P_{\text{ext},j}\leq\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j}\sum_{i}\frac{\omega}{2}|p_{j}^{\dagger}w_{i}|^{2}\underbrace{\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}\xi}\left(u_{i}^{\dagger}u_{i}-\frac{|u_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}|^{2}}{\operatorname{Im}\xi+v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}}\right)}_{f_{i}},

(G.123)

where we defined enhancement factor
f_{i}
(depends only on
n
and
j
). Using Eq. (
G.118
), we can show that:

\frac{\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j}P_{\text{ext},j}}{P_{0}}\leq f_{n=1,j=2},

(G.124)

where
P_{0}=\omega k^{3}p_{0}^{3}/12\pi
is the power radiated by a dipole with amplitude
p_{0}
in vacuum. Similarly, one can show that:

\frac{\rho_{\rm tot}}{\rho_{0}}\leq 1+f_{n=1,j=2}.

(G.125)

The enhancement factor
f_{n=1,j=2}
shows how large the light extinction of three uncorrelated dipoles can be, compared to the vacuum. While the first term in Eq. (
G.123
) appears in previous lossy-material bound
[
61
]
, the second term comes from radiation coupling between the bounding volume and the vacuum.
In near field when material loss dominates,
f_{n=1,j=2}
can be simplified to the lossy-material bound:

\displaystyle f_{n=1,j=2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}\xi}u_{i}^{\dagger}u_{i}

(G.126)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}\xi}\left(x-\frac{1}{x}-\frac{1}{x^{3}}\right)\bigg|_{kR_{1}}^{kR_{2}}

(G.127)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}\xi}\frac{1}{k^{3}R_{1}^{3}},

(G.128)

where, in the last line, we take the limit of extreme near field where
kR_{1}\ll 1,kR_{2}
.
In Fig.
G.4
, we showed the general bound and lossy-material bound for LDOS enhancement at wavelength
360
nm by Ag surroundings. It is clear that both bounds follow Eq. (
G.128
) in near field limit. In far field, general bound is slightly tighter than the lossy-material bound due to the consideration of additional radiative loss.

Figure G.4:
Bounding volume for the LDOS problem is chosen to be a spherical shell with three randomly oriented dipoles in the center, radiating at wavelength
\lambda=360
nm. Inner radius of the shell is determined by the minimum distance
d
to the scatterer comprising only Ag. Outer radius
R
of the shell covers the far end of the scatterer and is assumed to be one wavelength in the figure. In the far field, general bound is tighter than lossy-material bound. In the near field, general bound converge to lossy-material bound, and both follow the same divergence as
\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}\xi}\frac{1}{k^{3}d^{3}}
.

Absorption and scattering bounds can also be written through an enhancement factor over the vacuum radiation:

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j}P_{\text{abs},p_{j}}}{P_{0}}\leq f^{\rm abs}_{n=1,j=2}(\nu^{*})

(G.129)

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j}P_{\text{sca},p_{j}}}{P_{0}}\leq f^{\rm sca}_{n=1,j=2}(\nu^{*}).

(G.130)

Though they are more complicated in the sense that both enhancement factors (defined below) are functions of
\nu^{*}
, the optimal dual variable. Similarly, for non-radiative and radiative LDOS we can write:

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{\rm nr}}{\rho_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq f^{\rm abs}_{n=1,j=2}(\nu^{*})

(G.131)

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{\rm rad}}{\rho_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq 1+f^{\rm sca}_{n=1,j=2}(\nu^{*}).

(G.132)

Lastly, we present the explicit expressions of absorptive and scattering enhancement factors.
For absorption, the enhancement factor is:

f^{\rm abs}_{n=1,j=2}(\nu^{*})=\frac{\nu^{*2}}{4(\nu^{*}-1)}\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}\xi}\left(u_{i}^{\dagger}u_{i}-\frac{|u_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}|^{2}}{(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}\xi/\nu^{*}+v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}}\right),

where
\nu^{*}
is determined by solving
a=(\nu^{*}-1)\operatorname{Im}\xi/\nu^{*}
in the following equation:

\displaystyle 2a\left(u_{i}^{\dagger}u_{i}-\frac{|u_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}|^{2}}{a+v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}}\right)=\left\{u_{i}^{\dagger}u_{i}\operatorname{Im}\xi+|u_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}|^{2}\left[1-\frac{(\operatorname{Im}\xi+v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i})(2a+v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i})}{(a+v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i})^{2}}\right]\right\}.

For scattering, the enhancement factor is:

f^{\rm sca}_{n=1,j=2}(\nu^{*})=\frac{\nu^{*}}{4}\frac{1}{\operatorname{Im}\xi}\left(u_{i}^{\dagger}u_{i}-\frac{|u_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}|^{2}}{\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\xi/(\nu^{*}-1)+v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}}\right),

where
\nu^{*}
is determined by solving
a=\nu^{*}\operatorname{Im}\xi/(\nu^{*}-1)
in the following equation:

\displaystyle 2\operatorname{Im}\xi\left(u_{i}^{\dagger}u_{i}-\frac{|u_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}|^{2}}{a+v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}}\right)=\left\{u_{i}^{\dagger}u_{i}\operatorname{Im}\xi+|u_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i}|^{2}\left[1-\frac{(\operatorname{Im}\xi+v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i})(2a+v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i})}{(a+v_{i}^{\dagger}v_{i})^{2}}\right]\right\}.

G.14 Thermal absorption and emission

Our formalism applies equally to thermal absorption and emission. By Kirchhoff’s Law (reciprocity), or its nonreciprocal generalization
[
223
]
, total thermal absorption and emission are equivalent and can be found by considering a weighted average of incoherent, orthogonal incoming fields
e_{\textrm{inc},i}
:

\langle\left|e_{\rm inc}\right|^{2}\rangle=\sum_{i}w_{i}\left|e_{\text{inc},i}\right|^{2},

(G.133)

where
w_{i}
is a weighting factor. For a continuum of incoming fields the sum is instead an integral with a differential weight. A direct consequence of the incoherent averaging is that an upper bound to the average absorptivity/emissivity is given by the average of the bounds for each independent incident field. Surprisingly, the bounds computed by this averaging procedure varies depending on which basis is used for the incoming fields. If the incident field is treated as an incoherent sum of plane waves, over all propagation angles, for example, then the absorptivity/emissivity cross-section bounds would simply be a scalar multiple of Eq. (
G.85
). However, the bound can be tightened (decreased) if the incident fields are instead decomposed in vector spherical waves, for which the weight function
w_{i}
is determined by the fluctuation-dissipation theorem
[
334
]
:
w_{i}=\frac{4}{\pi\omega}\Theta(T)
, where
\Theta(T)=\hbar\omega/(e^{\hbar\omega/k_{B}T}-1)
is the Planck energy of a harmonic oscillator at temperature
T
without the zero-point energy. The resulting bound is a sum over all VSW channels
i
:

P_{\text{abs}}\leq\frac{2}{\pi}\Theta(T)\sum_{i}\begin{cases}\frac{\rho_{i}\operatorname{Im}\xi}{(\operatorname{Im}{\xi}+\rho_{i})^{2}}&\text{for }\rho_{i}\leq\operatorname{Im}{\xi}\\
\frac{1}{4}&\text{for }\rho_{i}\geq\operatorname{Im}{\xi}\end{cases}

(G.134)

where
i=\{n,m,j\}
includes all VSW channels:
n=1,2,...
,
m=-n,...,n
,
j=1,2
, and the sum converges for any nonzero
\operatorname{Im}\xi
. Eq. (
G.57
) shows a distinct threshold behavior within each VSW channel. In the asymptotic limits of radiation-dominant (
\rho_{i}\gg\operatorname{Im}\xi
) or material-loss-dominant (
\rho_{i}\ll\operatorname{Im}\xi
) scenarios, Eq. (
G.57
) simplifies to the known channel-
[
49
]
and lossy-material bounds
[
61
]
. In tandem, accounting for both mechanisms yields a significantly tighter bound than any previous approach.

Taking the same approach as in Sec.
2.3
in Chapter
2
, we compute the bound for finite-sized thermal absorbers with a spherical bounding volume. Figure
G.5
shows the thermal absorption/emission cross-section as a function of the size of a spherical silver
[
129
]
nanoparticle at wavelength
\lambda=$360\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}$
. Included is the bound of Eq. (
G.57
), which is nearly achieved by the sphere at its ideal resonant size. We also include the recently published
\mathbb{T}
-operator bound of Ref.
[
332
]
, which considered the effect of radiation and material losses separately for thermal sources. As shown in Fig.
G.5
, by incorporating both losses in one optical theorem constraint, even for thermal fields the new bounds are slightly tighter.
