Appendix H
Computational bounds via local power conservation: supplementary

This Appendix provides supplementary materials to support the arguments in Chapter
3
. We (1) show the standard semidefinite relaxation process of the QCQP that leads to computational bounds, (2) derive the maximally violated local constraints that are used in our iterative algorithm, (3) show the detailed evolution of the iterative algorithm in the exemplar case of the maximal absorption cross-section in cylindrical and triangle domains, (4) derive the expressions of the
T
matrix in cylindrical wave basis, (5) translate the expression from
T
matrix to
S
matrix and formulate
S
-matrix-feasibility objective as a quadratic function of polarization fields, (6) show the numerical details of the computational bounds that determine the minimum diameter of a power splitter, (7) formulate bandwidth-averaged extinction as a quadratic function of the polarization field at a complex frequency via contour integrals, and (8) prove the positive semidefiniteness of operators
\operatorname{Im}\xi
and
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}
at complex frequencies.

H.1 Semidefinite relaxation of the QCQP problem

In Eq. (
3.3
) of Chapter
3
, we show that one can formulate the bound problem with a quadratic-form objective and the conservation-law constraints:

\displaystyle\underset{p}{\text{max.}}

\displaystyle f(p)=p^{\dagger}\mathbb{A}p+\operatorname{Re}\left(\beta^{\dagger}p\right)+c

(H.1)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}

\displaystyle p^{\dagger}\operatorname{Re}\left\{\mathbb{D}_{j}(\xi+\mathbb{G}_{0})\right\}p=-\operatorname{Re}\left(p^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{j}e_{\rm inc}\right),\quad\text{for }i=1,2,...,2n.

Here we separate the real and imaginary parts of the complex-valued constraints in Eq. (
3.3
) by taking the real part of the original constraints in Eq. (
H.1
) and appending
n
more
\mathbb{D}
-matrix constraints with
\mathbb{D}_{n+j}=i\mathbb{D}_{j}
constraints to account for the imaginary part.
This type of problem with quadratic objective and quadratic constraints is well studied in the optimization literature
[
117
,
107
,
78
]
.
In this section we describe how it is translated to a semidefinite program using standard techniques: each step below is also clearly explained in Ref.
[
107
]
.
The first step of the transformation is to
homogenize
the quadratic forms on Eq. (
H.1
), which means introducing an additional variable in order to have purely quadratic and scalar terms without any linear term. To do this, in the objective function we introduce a complex-valued scalar variable
s
into the linear term:

\displaystyle f(p)=p^{\dagger}\mathbb{A}p+\operatorname{Re}\left(s^{\dagger}\beta^{\dagger}p\right)+c.

(H.2)

The key advantage of introducing this variable is that now one can write
f
as a homogeneous quadratic form:

\displaystyle f\left(\begin{bmatrix}p\\
s\end{bmatrix}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}p\\
s\end{pmatrix}^{\dagger}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{A}&\frac{1}{2}\beta\\
\frac{1}{2}\beta^{\dagger}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}p\\
s\end{pmatrix}+c.

(H.3)

We can do this for each of the
j
constraints as well, introducing the dummy variable s for each constraint, which then takes the form:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}p\\
s\end{pmatrix}^{\dagger}\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Re}\left\{\mathbb{D}_{j}(\xi+\mathbb{G}_{0})\right\}&\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{D}_{j}e_{\rm inc}\\
\frac{1}{2}\omega e_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{j}^{\dagger}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}p\\
s\end{pmatrix}=0.

(H.4)

One cannot allow
s
to take arbitrary values or else it will modify the initial problem. Instead, it should be required to have modulus one, i.e.,
|s|^{2}=1
, which is itself a quadratic form in the degrees of freedom
p
and
s
. Finally, we can lump all degrees of freedom into a single vector
v
:

\displaystyle v=\begin{pmatrix}p\\
s\end{pmatrix}.

(H.5)

With this notation, the objective, the
2n
conservation-law constraints, and the modulus constraint of
s
are all written in the form

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}\mathbb{F}v.

(H.6)

The way to optimize over such quadratic forms is to “lift” them to a higher-dimensional space where they become linear forms. The first step is to use the trace operator to rewrite the quadratic form:

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}\mathbb{F}v=\operatorname{Tr}{\mathbb{F}vv^{\dagger}}.

(H.7)

Then one defines a rank-one
matrix variable

\mathbb{X}
given by
vv^{\dagger}
, in which case we now have a linear form:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}{\mathbb{F}vv^{\dagger}}=\operatorname{Tr}{\mathbb{F}\mathbb{X}}.

(H.8)

One cannot optimize arbitrarily over
\mathbb{X}
and have an equivalent problem; one must additionally impose constraints that
\mathbb{X}
be a rank-one, positive-definite matrix. The rank-one constraint is nonconvex; the “relaxation” in semidefinite relaxation (SDR) refers to dropping this rank-one constraint. Once that constraint has been removed, one is left with a linear objective function (in
\mathbb{X}
), and
2n+1
linear constraints, over the space of positive-definite matrices. The transformation to a semidefinite program is complete.

The same transformation applies to the weighted average of the
\mathbb{D}_{j}
-matrix constraints as in Eq. (
H.9
) of Chapter
3
:

\displaystyle\underset{p}{\text{maximize}}

\displaystyle f(p)=p^{\dagger}\mathbb{A}p+\operatorname{Im}\left(\beta^{\dagger}p\right)

(H.9)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle p^{\dagger}\operatorname{Re}\left[\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},j}(\mathbb{G}_{0}+\xi\mathbb{I})\right]p=-\operatorname{Re}\left(p^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},j}e_{\rm inc}\right),\quad\text{for }j=1,2,...,m,

which essentially has the same form as Eq. (
H.1
). If there is only one (global) constraint in the optimization problem Eq. (
H.9
), the semidefinite relaxation mentioned in Eqs. (
H.2
)–(
H.8
) does not introduce any actual relaxation
[
107
]
. Furthermore, even though a certain degree of relaxation may be triggered by additional (local) constraints, it is straightforward to show that the additional constraints can
only
tighten the bound. This can also be seen in our examples for both absorption cross-section (Fig.
3.1
(b) in Chapter
3
) and broadband extinction (Fig.
3.2
in Chapter
3
), where the bounds are always monotonically decreasing with the additional local constraints.

H.2 Algorithm: Maximally violated local constraints

In this section we derive the optimal new
\mathbb{D}_{\rm wei}
matrix, and corresponding conservation-law constraint, that should be added to a given set of constraints by our principle of maximum violation. The conservation-law constraints as given in Eq. (
H.9
) are of the form

\displaystyle p^{\dagger}\operatorname{Re}\left[\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},j}(\mathbb{G}_{0}+\xi\mathbb{I})\right]p=-\operatorname{Re}\left(p^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},j}e_{\rm inc}\right),

(H.10)

where
j
runs from 1 to
m
, where
m
is the current number of constraints that have been imposed. The key remaining question, then, is how to select the
(m+1)^{\rm th}
constraint? From the first
m
constraints, one can identify a potentially optimal polarization field
p_{\rm opt}
as the first singular vector of the optimal matrix solution of the SDP (as discussed in Sec.
H
). Given this polarization field, then, a sensible approach to selecting a new constraint is to identify the constraint whose residual is largest when evaluated for polarization field
p_{\rm opt}
. In other words, we want the
\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},m+1}
that maximizes the quantity

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbb{D}}{\text{maximize}}

\displaystyle\left|\operatorname{Re}\left\{p_{\rm opt}^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}(\xi+\mathbb{G}_{0})p_{\rm opt}+p_{\rm opt}^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}e_{\rm inc}\right\}\right|.

(H.11)

By the cyclic property of the matrix trace, we can rewrite this expression as

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Tr}\left\{\mathbb{D}\left[(\xi+\mathbb{G}_{0})p_{\rm opt}p_{\rm opt}^{\dagger}+e_{\rm inc}p_{\rm opt}^{\dagger}\right]\right\},

(H.12)

where we dropped the absolute value since any optimal negative value can be reversed through
\mathbb{D}\rightarrow-\mathbb{D}
. Let us denote the matrix in square brackets as
\mathbb{C}
. Expanding the real (Hermitian) part, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\operatorname{Tr}\left(\mathbb{D}\mathbb{C}\right)+\operatorname{Tr}\left(\mathbb{D}^{\dagger}\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}\right)\right].

(H.13)

Clearly one can maximize the residual by allowing the norm of
\mathbb{D}
to be arbitrarily large, but that would not give insight into which spatial pattern
\mathbb{D}
should take. As a normalization we can take the Frobenius norm of
\mathbb{D}
to be 1, i.e.
\operatorname{Tr}\left(\mathbb{D}^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}\right)=1
. Then, straightforward variational calculus yields an optimal
\mathbb{D}
matrix given by
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}
; since
\mathbb{D}
must be (spatially) diagonal, we take
\mathbb{D}
to comprise the diagonal elements of
\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},m+1}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{diag}\left[\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}\right]

\displaystyle=\omega\operatorname{diag}\left[p_{\rm opt}p_{\rm opt}^{\dagger}\left(\xi+\mathbb{G}_{0}\right)^{\dagger}+p_{\rm opt}e_{\rm inc}\right],

(H.14)

where now “
\operatorname{diag}
” strips its matrix argument of all elements except along the (spatial) diagonal, as in Chapter
3
. This is the optimal selection of the
\mathbb{D}_{\rm wei}
matrix as presented in Eq. (
3.5
) of Chapter
3
, which significantly accelerates convergence of the bound computation.

Figure H.1:
(a) The left panel shows spatial profiles of the global and the first four maximally-violated local constraints. The design region is a cylinder with diameter
0.18\lambda
and the presented profiles are its 2D cross sections. D
i
denotes the
E_{z}
component of
\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},i}
in space, normalized so its maximal magnitude is one. Each iteration generates an optimal current, as indicated by the direction of the arrows. The normalized
E_{z}
component of the optimal currents are shown on the right panel, which in turn generate the next maximally-violated local constraints. (b) Convergence of the upper bound on absorption cross-section,
\sigma_{\rm abs}
, under maximally-violated local constraints (red line) as compared to the ones from only the global constraints (blue line) or other type of local constraints (green and purple lines). Figures (c,d) are the same as (a,b) but the designableregion is now an equilateral triangle with side length 0.18
\lambda
.

H.3 Maximal absorption cross-section under local constraints

In Fig.
3.1
(b) of Chapter
3
, we provide an example of maximizing absorption cross-section under local constraints. In this section, we provide detail on the formulation of the optimization problem and the iteration process involved in identifying the maximally-violated local conservation laws. The main result in this section is summarized in Fig.
H.1
, where we consider not only a cylindrical design region, but also a triangular design region to showcase the generality of this computational approach.

The exact expression of absorption cross-section in terms of the polarization field
p
can be identified from the global power-conservation law
[
335
,
71
,
171
]
, which can be derived from Eq. (
H.10
) by choosing
\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},j}
as an identity multiplied by the unit imaginary number and multiplying Eq. (
H.10
) by a factor of
\omega/2
:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{\omega}{2}p^{\dagger}\left(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{G}_{0}\right)p}_{P_{\rm scat}}+\underbrace{\frac{\omega}{2}p^{\dagger}\left(\operatorname{Im}\xi\right)p}_{P_{\rm abs}}=\underbrace{\frac{\omega}{2}\operatorname{Im}\left(\psi_{\rm inc}^{\dagger}p\right)}_{P_{\rm ext}},

(H.15)

where each term from left to right represents scattered power, absorption, and extinction, respectively. If the incident wave is a plane wave, the expression of its intensity in our dimensionless unit is
I_{0}=|E_{0}|^{2}/2
, where
E_{0}
is the plane-wave amplitude. The absorption cross-section is defined as the ratio between absorption and plane-wave intensity
\sigma_{\rm abs}=P_{\rm abs}/I_{0}=\omega p^{\dagger}\left(\operatorname{Im}\xi\right)p/|E_{0}|^{2}
.

Maximizing absorption cross-section under local conservation laws is equivalent to the optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{p}{\text{max.}}

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm abs}=\omega p^{\dagger}\left(\operatorname{Im}\xi\right)p/|E_{0}|^{2}

(H.16)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}

\displaystyle p^{\dagger}\operatorname{Re}\left\{\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},j}(\xi+\mathbb{G}_{0})\right\}p=-\operatorname{Re}\left(p^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},j}e_{\rm inc}\right).\quad\quad j=1,2,...,m

Given designable region, incident field, and material properties as inputs, one solves this optimization problem via semidefinite relaxation discussed in Sec.
H
. The rest of this section considers a specific example where the incident wave is a TE-polarized plane wave and the material is nonmagnetic with susceptibility
\varepsilon=12+0.1i
. We consider two designable regions: a cylinder and a equilateral triangle. The cylindrical design region has diameter
d=0.18\lambda
, total length
h
in its translational invariant direction (
h\rightarrow\infty
, so we can solve the 2D simplification), and a geometric cross section
\sigma_{\rm geo}=dh
. The equilateral triangle has side length
l=0.18\lambda
, total length
h
in its translational invariant direction, and a geometric cross section
\sigma_{\rm geo}=\sqrt{3}/2lh
.

As mentioned in Sec.
H
, the algorithm for generating the maximally-violated local conservation constraints is built up from the existing global conservation constraints.
Thus, we first solve the optimization problem (
H.16
) with only the global real-power conservation constraint where
m=1
and
\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},1}=iI
. For a cylindrical design region, the optimization program returns an optimal polarization field
p_{1}
shown in the right panel of Fig.
H.1
(a), and an upper bound
\sigma_{\rm abs}/\sigma_{\rm geo}=4.12
, too loose to be shown in Fig.
H.1
(b). Adding an additional global reactive-power conservation constraint (
m=2
,
\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},1}=iI
, and
\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},2}=I
) gives us a dipole-like optimal current
p_{2}
shown in Fig.
H.1
(a), and an upper bound
\sigma_{\rm abs}/\sigma_{\rm geo}=0.139
, marked by the blue line in Fig.
H.1
(b). Next, we include extra local conservation laws in the optimization problem (
H.16
) to tighten the global bound (result shown in Fig.
H.1
(b)). In particular, we use the algorithm derived in Sec.
H
of this Appendix to generate maximally-violated local conservation constraints. For example, we use Eq. (
H.14
) to find out a local constraint,
\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},3}
, that is maximally violated by the optimal current
p_{2}
. The spatial profile of its diagonal components (denoted by D
3
) are shown in the left panel of Fig.
H.1
(a). This additional constraint reduces the upper bound (the second red marker from the left in Fig.
H.1
(b)), and together with global constraints
\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},1}
and
\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei},2}
, predicts an optimal current
p_{3}
which resembles the polarization field in an unpatterned cylinder.
We continue this iteration for 50 more times in Fig.
H.1
(b) and show the spatial profile of the first six in Fig.
H.1
(a). After the fourth iteration, both the upper bound (red line Fig.
H.1
(b)), and the optimal currents have converged to the solution of an unstructured cylinder, suggesting the ineffectiveness of structuring in this particular case.

The same algorithm is applied to a equilateral triangular design region shown in Fig.
H.1
(c,d). In this example, we consider two possible scattering structures: an unpatterned triangle with a dimension the same as the design region, and the largest unpatterned cylinder that can fit in the design region (bottom panel of Fig.
H.1
(c)). Neither structure generates the optimal current distribution predicted in the right panel of Fig.
H.1
(c), and consistently, neither reach the predicted upper bound in Fig.
H.1
(d). Unlike a cylindrical design region where an unpatterned cylinder is already the optimum, a triangular design region may benefit from a more complex structure. From a computational perspective, the asymmetry of the triangular region has no effect on the speed or convergence of the bound computations.

H.4 Volume integral form of T-matrix

In Sec.
3.3
of Chapter
3
, one of the examples considered is whether a specific scattering-matrix can be targeted by some designable region, an example that we discuss more in the next section. In this section, in preparation for that, we derive the transition-matrix (
T
-matrix) elements for waves impinging upon and exiting from a 2D circular bounding region. The
T
-matrix calculation is simpler than a direct
S
-matrix calculation, and the two are related in a simple way, as noted in the next section.

We first derive the volume integral form of
T
-matrix elements as a linear function of the polarization field in arbitrary basis functions in six-vector notations (i.e.,
\psi
for fields,
\phi
for sources,
\Gamma_{0}
for the background Green’s function operator). Then, specifically for a 2D circular bounding region, we derive the
T
-matrix expression in the basis of vector cylindrical waves.

Given arbitrary bounding volume
V
, a set of incoming basis
\{\psi_{\rm in,n}\}
is defined on its surface
\partial V
through the orthogonal relation:

-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\partial V}\psi_{\rm in,i}(\bm{x}_{s})^{\dagger}P(\bm{x}_{s})\psi_{\rm in,j}(\bm{x}_{s})=\delta_{ij},\quad\quad P=\begin{pmatrix}0&\hat{\bm{n}}\times\\
-\hat{\bm{n}}\times&0\end{pmatrix},

(H.17)

with
\hat{\bm{n}}
being the unit normal vector. When
i=j
, the right hand side of the orthogonality relation measures the power flow of state
\psi_{\rm in,i}
through the surface
\partial V
. (We choose the convention pointing outward for outgoing states and inward for incoming states.)
Outgoing states can be defined as the time reverse of the incoming states:

\psi_{\rm out,i}(\bm{x}_{s})=Q\psi^{*}_{\rm in,i}(\bm{x}_{s}),\quad\quad Q=\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
0&-I\end{pmatrix},

(H.18)

where the operator
Q
flips the sign of the magnetic field, as required by time reversing.
The incident basis
\{\psi_{\rm inc,i}\}
is defined by a linear combination of the incoming and outgoing basis:

\psi_{\rm inc,i}=\alpha\psi_{\rm out,i}+\beta\psi_{\rm in,i}.

Coefficients
\alpha
and
\beta
depend on the exact basis one choose. For example, for vector cylindrical waves, they are both
\frac{1}{2}
.

Incident field
\psi_{\rm inc}
can be expanded by the incident basis with coefficients
c_{\rm inc,i}
.
Similarly, scattered field
\psi_{\rm out}
can be expanded by the outgoing basis with coefficients
c_{\rm out,i}
. These two sets of coefficients are connected by
T
-matrix.

\psi_{\rm inc}=\sum_{i}c_{\rm inc,i}\psi_{\rm inc,i},\quad\quad\psi_{\rm scat}=\sum_{i}c_{\rm out,i}\psi_{\rm out,i},\quad\quad\begin{pmatrix}\\
c_{\rm out}\\
\ \end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\\
T\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\\
c_{\rm inc}\\
\ \end{pmatrix}.

(H.19)

Thus, the entry
T_{ij}
measures the ratio
c_{\rm out,i}/c_{\rm inc,j}
. In other words, when the incident field
\psi_{\rm inc}=\psi_{\rm inc,i}
,
T_{ij}
takes the value of
c_{\rm out,i}
. Using this definition, we can express
T_{ij}
as a linear function of polarization field
\phi
after some mathematical manipulation:

\displaystyle T_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\partial V}\psi_{\rm out,i}^{\dagger}(\bm{x}_{s})P(\bm{x}_{s})\psi_{\rm scat}(\bm{x}_{s})

(H.20)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\alpha}\int_{\partial V}\psi_{\rm inc,i}^{\dagger}(\bm{x}_{s})P(\bm{x}_{s})\psi_{\rm scat}(\bm{x}_{s}),

(H.21)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\alpha}\int_{\partial V}\int_{V}\psi_{\rm inc,i}^{\dagger}(\bm{x}_{s})P(\bm{x}_{s})\Gamma_{0}(\bm{x}_{s},\bm{x}_{v})\phi(\bm{x}_{v})

(H.22)

where we used the fact that
\psi_{\rm out,i}=\frac{1}{\alpha}\psi_{\rm inc,i}-\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\psi_{\rm in}
, and the incoming and outgoing fields are orthogonal in this inner product. To further simplify this equation, we first take its transpose, and then use the properties
P^{T}(\bm{x}_{s})=P(\bm{x}_{s})
and
\Gamma_{0}^{T}(\bm{x}_{s},\bm{x}_{v})=\Gamma_{0}(\bm{x}_{v},\bm{x}_{s})
to write
T_{ij}
as:

\displaystyle T_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\alpha}\int_{\partial V}\int_{V}\phi^{T}(\bm{x}_{v})\Gamma_{0}(\bm{x}_{v},\bm{x}_{s})P(\bm{x}_{s})\psi_{\rm inc,i}^{*}(\bm{x}_{s})

(H.23)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\alpha}\int_{\partial V}\int_{V}\phi^{T}(\bm{x}_{v})\Gamma_{0}(\bm{x}_{v},\bm{x}_{s})P(\bm{x}_{s})\psi_{\rm inc,i}(\bm{x}_{s}),

(H.24)

where we use the properties
\psi_{\rm inc,i}^{*}(\bm{x}_{s})=Q\psi_{\rm inc,i}(\bm{x}_{s})
and
-P(\bm{x}_{s})Q=P(\bm{x}_{s})
to derive the second equality. Lastly, we identify that the product
P(\bm{x}_{s})\psi_{\rm inc,i}(\bm{x}_{s})
gives the surface equivalent current
\xi_{\rm inc,i}(\bm{x}_{s})
on the surface
\partial V
, which can be propagated back to the volume through the Green’s function:

\displaystyle T_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\alpha}\int_{\partial V}\int_{V}\phi^{T}(\bm{x}_{v})\Gamma_{0}(\bm{x}_{v},\bm{x}_{s})\xi_{\rm inc,i}(\bm{x}_{s})

(H.25)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\alpha}\int_{V}\phi^{T}(\bm{x}_{v})\psi_{\rm inc,i}(\bm{x}_{v})

(H.26)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\alpha}\phi^{T}\psi_{\rm inc,i}.

(H.27)

The key result, Eq. (
H.27
), identifies
T_{ij}
as a overlap integral between incident channel
\psi_{\rm inc,i}
and polarization field
\phi
that is induced by incident field
\psi_{\rm inc,j}
.

For a highly symmetric bounding volume, the derivation of the volume integral form of
T
-matrix can be greatly simplified. In the example provided in Sec.
3.3
of Chapter
3
, we assume nonmagnetic material with a 2D bounding area and TE incidence. The basis for outgoing and incident field can be chosen as the set of vector cylindrical waves:

\displaystyle v_{\rm inc,n}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\hat{z}J_{n}(k\rho)e^{in\phi}

(H.28)

\displaystyle v_{\rm out,n}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\hat{z}H^{(1)}_{n}(k\rho)e^{in\phi},

(H.29)

where
J_{n}(x)
is the Bessel function of order
n
, and
H^{(1)}_{n}(x)
is the Hankel function of the first kind of order
n
. Conventionally, these two basis written here do not include magnetic field, and are not normalized based on Eq. (
H.17
), so we use different notations other than
\psi_{\rm inc,n}
and
\psi_{\rm out,n}
.

As discussed before, when
\psi_{\rm inc}=v_{\rm out,j}
, the entry
T_{ij}=c_{\text{scat},i}
. By virtue of the Green’s function expansion
\Gamma_{0}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=i\sum_{n}v_{\rm out,n}(\bm{x})v_{\rm inc,n}^{\dagger}(\bm{x}^{\prime})
for
\rho>\rho^{\prime}
, we can easily derive the volume integral form of
T_{ij}
:

\displaystyle T_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\partial V}v^{\dagger}_{\rm out,i}(\bm{x}_{s})\psi_{\rm scat}(\bm{x}_{s})\text{d}S}{\int_{\partial V}v^{\dagger}_{\rm out,i}(\bm{x}_{s})v_{\rm out,i}(\bm{x}_{s})\text{d}S}

(H.30)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\partial V}v^{\dagger}_{\rm out,i}(\bm{x}_{s})\left[\int_{V}\Gamma_{0}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})\phi(\bm{x}^{\prime})\text{d}V\right]\text{d}S}{\int_{\partial V}v^{\dagger}_{\rm out,i}(\bm{x}_{s})v_{\rm out,i}(\bm{x}_{s})\text{d}S}

(H.31)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\partial V}v^{\dagger}_{\rm out,i}(\bm{x}_{s})\left[\int_{V}i\left(\sum_{n}v_{\rm out,n}(\bm{x})v_{\rm inc,n}^{\dagger}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\right)\phi(\bm{x})\text{d}V\right]\text{d}S}{\int_{\partial V}v^{\dagger}_{\rm out,i}(\bm{x}_{s})v_{\rm out,i}(\bm{x}_{s})\text{d}S}

(H.32)

\displaystyle=i\int_{V}v_{\rm inc,i}^{\dagger}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\phi(\bm{x}^{\prime})\text{d}V

(H.33)

\displaystyle=iv_{\rm inc,i}^{\dagger}\phi.

(H.34)

Similar as before, the result suggests that
T_{ij}
is the projection of
\phi
into the given incident basis
v_{\rm inc,i}
with an additional phase delay, under the incident field
\psi_{\rm inc}=v_{\rm inc,j}
. There is slight difference between this and the more general result in Eq. (
H.27
) because the vector cylindrical waves defined in equations (
H.28
) and (
H.29
) do not include magnetic field components and are not normalized based on Eq. (
H.17
).

H.5 Formulation of S-matrix feasibility bound

The objective for the S-matrix feasibility problem is to minimize the relative difference between the achievable and target
S
matrices:

\text{Min\ \ }f_{\rm obj}=\left\|S-S_{\rm target}\right\|^{2}/\left\|S_{\rm target}\right\|^{2},

(H.35)

where we choose
\|\cdot\|
to denote Frobenius norm.

It is simpler to translate the scattering matrix
S
, which relates
incoming
waves to
outgoing
waves, into the transition matrix
T
, which relates
incident
waves to
scattered
waves. One can typically choose a basis (such as the cylindrical-wave basis) for which
S=I+2T
. Inserting this relation into Eq. (
H.35
), we have:

\displaystyle f_{\rm obj}

\displaystyle=4\left\|T-T_{\rm target}\right\|/\left\|S_{\rm target}\right\|^{2}

(H.36)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{||S_{\rm target}||^{2}}\sum_{ij}|T_{ij}-T_{\text{target},ij}|^{2}

(H.37)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{||S_{\rm target}||^{2}}\sum_{j}f_{\text{obj},j},

(H.38)

where in the last equality we separate out the objective into contributions from different incident fields:

\displaystyle f_{\rm obj,j}=\sum_{i}|T_{ij}-T_{\text{target},ij}|^{2}.

(H.39)

Each
f_{\rm obj,j}
corresponds to the scattering from incident field indexed by
j
, so we bound them separately and later add up their contributions. As we proved in Sec.
H
,
T_{ij}
can be written as a linear function of
\phi
, which is the induced polarization field under the incident field
\psi_{\rm inc}=\psi_{\rm inc,j}
. Assume this linear relation is
T_{ij}=w_{i}^{\dagger}\phi
. We can plug it in Eq. (
H.39
) to express each
f_{\rm obj,j}
as a quadratic function of
\phi
:

\displaystyle f_{\rm obj,j}=\phi^{\dagger}\left(\sum_{i}w_{i}w_{i}^{\dagger}\right)\phi+\operatorname{Re}\left[\left(-2\sum_{i}T_{\text{target},ij}w_{i}\right)^{\dagger}\phi\right]+\sum_{i}|T_{\text{target},ij}|^{2},

(H.40)

This can be written in the form of the objective in Eq. (
3.3
) of Chapter
3
(after adding a minus sign to the objective to turn minimization into maximization) with
\mathbb{A}=-\sum_{i}w_{i}w_{i}^{\dagger}
,
\beta=2\sum_{i}T_{\text{target},ij}w_{i}
,
c=-\sum_{i}|T_{\text{target},ij}|^{2}
, and
\psi_{\rm inc}=\psi_{\rm inc,j}
.

For the general case where the incident basis
\psi_{\rm inc,i}
is defined through Eq. (
H.17
), we substitute
\omega_{i}
in Eq. (
H.40
) with
\psi_{\rm inc,i}^{*}/(4\alpha^{*})
. For the specific case where the we assume nonmagnetic material with a 2D bounding area and TE incidence, the incident basis
\psi_{\rm inc,i}
is vector cylindrical waves
v_{\rm inc,i}
defined in Eq. (
H.28
), and we substitute
\omega_{i}
with
-iv_{\rm inc,i}
.

Figure H.2:
(a) Minimum diameter required for a power splitter for a single input to 2M + 1 outgoing channels. (b) Lower bounds on the objective
||S-S_{\rm target}||^{2}/||S_{\rm target}||^{2}
at each diameter when the largest scattering channel
M=5
. Uncertainties rising from the numerical instabilities in global constraints are marked by the dotted blue lines.

H.6 Minimum diameter of a power splitter

In Sec.
3.3
of Chapter
3
, we show the minimum diameter required for a power splitter for a single input to 2M + 1 outgoing channels in the cylindrical-wave basis. The way we determine the minimum diameter for each
M
is to minimize the objective function
\left\|S-S_{\rm target}\right\|^{2}/\left\|S_{\rm target}\right\|^{2}
for every diameter
d
, and choose the smallest one that satisfies
\left\|S-S_{\rm target}\right\|^{2}/\left\|S_{\rm target}\right\|^{2}<1\%
. This process is shown in Fig.
H.2
(b) for the case with
M=5
.

The gap between two blue lines in Fig.
H.2
(b) originates from a numerical instability in the global-constraint-only approach. Higher orders of the cylindrical waves yield widely separated numerical scales in the corresponding matrices, such that with only global constraints the optimization does not terminate successfully for some diameters. The two dashed blue lines indicate the uncertainty region for determining the minimum diameter. The lower bound of this uncertainty region is estimated from the asymptotic limit of the global-constraint-only approach in Fig.
H.2
(a). The minimum diameter can be lower bounded by the lower dashed line of the uncertainty region, which explains the location of the circular point with the errorbar.

H.7 Formulation of the bandwidth-averaged extinction bound

In this section, we transform the bandwidth-averaged extinction to a single scattering amplitude at a complex frequency by Cauchy’s residue theorem, using a similar technique to that which has been demonstrated in Refs.
[
187
,
186
]
.
We start with the expression of single-frequency extinction cross section at a real frequency:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm ext}(\omega)=\operatorname{Im}\left[\omega e^{\dagger}_{\rm inc}(\omega)p(\omega)\right]

(H.41)

Incident field
e_{\rm inc}(\omega)
in far-field scattering is often approximated as a plane wave. Without loss of generality, we assume it has unit intensity and is propagating along the
x
direction. We use dimensionless quantities with
c=1
, so the plane-wave frequency dependence can be written as
e^{i\omega x}
. In anticipation of an analytic continuation into the complex plane, we use the general relation
e_{\rm inc}^{*}(\omega)=e_{\rm inc}(-\omega)
for real-valued frequencies
[
336
]
to remove the complex conjugation (which cannot be analytically continued):

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm ext}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Im}\left[\omega e^{T}_{\rm inc}(-\omega)p(\omega)\right]

(H.42)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Im}s(\omega).

(H.43)

Here, we define a new term
s(\omega)=\omega e^{T}_{\rm inc}(-\omega)p(\omega)
that we identify as the far-field scattering amplitude.
Since the incident plane wave
e_{\rm inc}(\omega)
has the frequency dependence
e^{i\omega x}
(analytic everywhere), and the polarization field
p(\omega)
is a causal linear-response function
[
337
]
, the amplitude
s(\omega)
is analytic in the upper half of the complex-frequency plane (UHP).

The average extinction cross section
\langle\sigma_{\rm ext}\rangle
in a bandwidth
\Delta\omega
around a center frequency
\omega_{0}
can be defined as the integral of the product of
\sigma_{\rm ext}(\omega)
and a Lorentzian window function
H_{\omega_{0},\Delta\omega}(\omega)=\frac{\Delta\omega/\pi}{(\omega-\omega_{0})^{2}+\Delta\omega^{2}}
:

\displaystyle\langle\sigma_{\rm ext}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\sigma_{\rm ext}(\omega)H(\omega)\text{d}\omega

(H.44)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Im}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}s(\omega)H(\omega)\text{d}\omega

(H.45)

The integrand
s(\omega)H(\omega)
has two properties that allows us to use Cauchy’s residue theorem to equate
the all-frequency integral to a single pole in the UHP. The first property is that the
s(\omega)H(\omega)
only has one pole
\tilde{\omega}=\omega_{0}+i\Delta\omega
from the window function in the UHP, since
s(\omega)
is complex analytic in the UHP as discussed above. The second property is the magnitude of
s(\omega)H(\omega)
decays faster than
1/|\omega|
when
|\omega|\rightarrow+\infty
. In this asymptotic limit, the window function
H(\omega)
decays at a rate of
1/|\omega|
, and the amplitude
s(\omega)
decay at the rate of
1/|\omega|
, which can be proved as follows.

In the high-frequency limit, the polarization field must decay towards zero (the bound charges cannot respond to
such high frequencies), and on physical grounds
[
336
]
the decay must occur in proportion to
1/|\omega|
. Conventionally, the decay constant is chosen to be a “plasma frequency”
\omega_{p}
that is physically meaningful for metals but applies to dielectrics as well. Because the scatterer becomes transparent at high frequencies, the Born approximation applies and the polarization field will be directly proportional to the incident field:
p(|\omega|\rightarrow\infty)=-\frac{\omega_{p}^{2}}{\omega^{2}}e_{\rm inc}(\omega)
, so that
s(|\omega|\rightarrow\infty)=-\frac{\omega_{p}^{2}}{\omega}e_{\rm inc}^{T}(-\omega)e_{\rm inc}(\omega)\sim 1/\omega
. Note that the inner product
e_{\rm inc}^{T}(-\omega)e_{\rm inc}(\omega)
does not dependent on frequency as the frequency dependence of the incident plane wave is just
e^{i\omega x}
.

Taking these two properties into account, we can connect the upper and lower limit of the integral in Eq. (
H.44
) by a half circle in the UHP, which does not actually contribute to the integral due to the fast decay rate of the
s(\omega)H(\omega)
. Integration of this closed loop can be transformed into the single pole of
s(\omega)H(\omega)
at
\tilde{\omega}=\omega_{0}+i\Delta\omega
by Cauchy’s residue theorem, giving the expression in Chapter
3
:

\langle\sigma_{\rm ext}\rangle=\operatorname{Im}\left[\tilde{\omega}e^{T}_{\rm inc}(-\tilde{\omega})p(\tilde{\omega})\right].

(H.46)

In the case of TE incidence in a 2D geometry with nonmagnetic material, we only need to consider the
z
polarization component of the electric incident field, which is a scalar quantity. If we still use notation
e_{\rm inc}
to denote this quantity, we can solve for the maximum
\langle\sigma_{\rm ext}\rangle
by the optimization problem with
\beta=i\tilde{\omega}^{*}e^{i\omega_{0}x+\Delta\omega x}
and incident field
e_{\rm inc}(\tilde{\omega})=e^{i\omega_{0}x-\Delta\omega x}
.

H.8 Positive semidefinite property of scattering and absorption operators

The power-bandwidth limit discussed in Sec.
3.4
of Chapter
3
relies on the fact that the local power-conservation laws can be extended to complex frequency
\omega
. Explicitly writing out the frequency dependency of the operators, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{\omega^{*}}{2}p^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei}}\mathbb{G}_{0}(\omega)p+\frac{\omega^{*}}{2}p^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei}}\xi(\omega)p=-\frac{\omega^{*}}{2}p^{\dagger}\mathbb{D}e_{\rm inc}.

(H.47)

Among all the possible local conservation laws we can impose, the most important one is the global power-conservation law. It constrains the optimization variable
p
to the boundary of a high-dimensional ellipsoid, and can be derived by assigning
\mathbb{D}_{\textrm{wei}}
an identity tensor and take the imaginary part of Eq. (
H.47
):

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}p^{\dagger}\operatorname{Im}\left[\omega^{*}\mathbb{G}_{0}(\omega)\right]p+\frac{1}{2}p^{\dagger}\operatorname{Im}\left[\omega^{*}\xi(\omega)\right]p=-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Im}(\omega^{*}p^{\dagger}e_{\rm inc}).

(H.48)

In this section, we prove the positive semidefinite property of the two involving operators,
\operatorname{Im}\{\omega^{*}\mathbb{G}_{0}(\omega)\}
and
\operatorname{Im}\{\omega^{*}\xi(\omega)\}
, in the UHP, using a similar technique to that which has been used in Ref.
[
338
,
339
,
116
]
.

We first prove the positive semidefinite property of the operator
\operatorname{Im}\{\omega^{*}\xi(\omega)\}
in a passive scattering problem. Passivity requires that the polarization fields
p
in the material do not do work. The total work they do up to a time
t
must be greater than or equal to zero:

\operatorname{Re}\int{\rm d}x\,\int_{-\infty}^{t}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\,e^{\dagger}(x,t^{\prime})\frac{\text{d}p(x,t^{\prime})}{\text{d}t^{\prime}}\geq 0.

(H.49)

In a scattering problem where
e
is the total field, we can interpret
p
as the polarization fields, which are the convolution of the susceptibility in time and space (we allow for spatial nonlocality):

\displaystyle p(x,t^{\prime})=\int{\rm d}x^{\prime}\int_{-\infty}^{t^{\prime}}{\rm d}t^{\prime\prime}\,\chi(x,x^{\prime},t^{\prime}-t^{\prime\prime})e(x^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})=\int{\rm d}x^{\prime}\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(x,x^{\prime},\tau)e(x^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\tau)\,{\rm d}\tau,

(H.50)

where in the second expression the variable
\tau
can be interpreted as the delay since the excitation that is creating a response. Inserting the latter expression into Eq. (
H.49
) we have:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\int\int{\rm d}x^{\prime}{\rm d}x\,\int_{-\infty}^{t}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\,e^{\dagger}(x,t^{\prime})\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}\tau\,\chi(x,x^{\prime},\tau)e^{\prime}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\tau)\geq 0,

(H.51)

where
e^{\prime}
denotes the derivative of
e
. The expression of Eq. (
H.51
) must be valid for all
e
. We can choose a simple time-dependence for
e
, following Refs.
[
339
,
116
]
:

\displaystyle e(x,t^{\prime})=\begin{cases}e(x)e^{-i\omega t^{\prime}}&\text{ for }t^{\prime}<T\\
0&\text{ for }t^{\prime}\geq T,\end{cases}

(H.52)

where
\omega
is a complex-valued frequency, i.e.
\omega=\omega_{0}+i\operatorname{Im}\omega
, and
T
is simply a shut-off time that we will always choose larger than
t
and which assures technical conditions are satisfied in rigorous proofs
[
339
,
116
]
. Given this form, Eq. (
H.51
) becomes:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\int\int{\rm d}x^{\prime}{\rm d}x\,e^{\dagger}(x)\int_{-\infty}^{t}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\,e^{i\omega_{0}t^{\prime}}e^{(\operatorname{Im}\omega)t^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}\tau\,\chi(x,x^{\prime},\tau)\left(-i\omega\right)e^{-i\omega_{0}(t^{\prime}-\tau)}e^{(\operatorname{Im}\omega)(t^{\prime}-\tau)}e(x^{\prime})\geq 0,

(H.53)

Re-arranging terms then gives

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\left[\left(-i\omega\right)\int\int{\rm d}x^{\prime}{\rm d}x\,e^{\dagger}(x)\int_{-\infty}^{t}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\,e^{2(\operatorname{Im}\omega)t^{\prime}}\left\{\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}\tau\,\chi(x,x^{\prime},\tau)e^{i\omega\tau}\right\}e(x^{\prime})\right]\geq 0.

(H.54)

The term in curly brackets is proportional to the Fourier transform of
\chi
, i.e.
\chi(x,x^{\prime},\omega)
at complex frequency
\omega
, and we can drop the constants related to
2\pi
. The integral over
t^{\prime}
is easily evaluated. Finally, noting that
\operatorname{Re}(-iz)=\operatorname{Im}(z)
, we have the expression

\displaystyle\frac{e^{2(\operatorname{Im}\omega)t}}{2\operatorname{Im}\omega}\operatorname{Im}\int\int{\rm d}x^{\prime}{\rm d}x\,e^{\dagger}(x)\left[\omega\chi(x,x^{\prime},\omega)\right]e(x^{\prime})\geq 0.

(H.55)

This expression must be valid for all
e(x)
distributions. We can remove the spatial dependence in
\chi(x,x^{\prime},\omega)
and instead treat it as a square matrix
\chi(\omega)
(as in any standard discretization), in which case we can simply write that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\left[\omega\chi(\omega)\right]\geq 0\quad\text{ for }\operatorname{Im}\omega>0,

(H.56)

where the imaginary part of the matrix argument refers to its anti-Hermitian part; e.g.,
\operatorname{Im}A=(A-A^{\dagger})/2i
.

To convert Eq. (
H.56
) to an inequality for
\xi(\omega)
, we use the fact that
\chi(\omega)=-\xi^{-1}(\omega)
to rewrite Eq. (
H.56
) as

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\left[\omega\chi(\omega)\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Im}\left[-\omega\xi^{-1}(\omega)\right]

(H.57)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Im}\left[-\omega\left(\xi^{\dagger}(\omega)\xi(\omega)\right)^{-1}\xi^{\dagger}(\omega)\right]

(H.58)

\displaystyle=\left(\xi^{\dagger}(\omega)\xi(\omega)\right)^{-1}\left(\operatorname{Im}\left[\omega^{*}\xi(\omega)\right]\right),

(H.59)

which implies that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\left[\omega^{*}\xi(\omega)\right]\geq 0\quad\text{ for }\operatorname{Im}\omega>0.

(H.60)

Thus we have our proof for the positive semidefinite property of the first of our two operators. Now we can follow similar logic for the second one. We start with an expression similar to Eq. (
H.49
), but now we change our interpretation: we will take the
p
to be free currents,
e
to be the fields radiated by them, and the quantity in Eq. (
H.49
) then represents the
negative
of the work done by those currents on the outgoing field (which again must be positive). Thus our starting point is the negative of Eq. (
H.49
):

\operatorname{Re}\left[-\int{\rm d}x\,\int_{-\infty}^{t}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\,\frac{\text{d}p(x,t^{\prime})}{\text{d}t^{\prime}}^{\dagger}e(x,t^{\prime})\right]\geq 0,

(H.61)

where we also reversed the order of our arguments in the integrand for simplicity below. (That is allowed because
\operatorname{Re}z=\operatorname{Re}z^{*}
.)

Now our convolution relation will connect the fields at a time
t^{\prime}
to the polarization fields at an earlier time through the background Green’s function:

\displaystyle e(x,t^{\prime})=\int{\rm d}x^{\prime}\int_{-\infty}^{t^{\prime}}{\rm d}t^{\prime\prime}\,\mathbb{G}_{0}(x,x^{\prime},t^{\prime}-t^{\prime\prime})p(x^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})=\int{\rm d}x^{\prime}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{G}_{0}(x,x^{\prime},\tau)p(x^{\prime},t^{\prime}-\tau)\,{\rm d}\tau.

(H.62)

We are going to insert this convolution relation into Eq. (
H.61
), analogous to what we did before. We can combine this with the step of specifying a time-dependence for the function
p(x,t)
:

\displaystyle p(x,t^{\prime})=\begin{cases}p(x)e^{-i\omega t^{\prime}}&\text{ for }t^{\prime}<T\\
0&\text{ for }t^{\prime}\geq T.\end{cases}

(H.63)

Performing these two steps in Eq. (
H.61
) we have:

\operatorname{Re}\int\int{\rm d}x{\rm d}x^{\prime}\,\int_{-\infty}^{t}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\,(-i\omega^{*})e^{i\omega_{0}t^{\prime}}e^{(\operatorname{Im}\omega)t^{\prime}}p^{\dagger}(x)\int{\rm d}\tau\,\mathbb{G}_{0}(x,x^{\prime},\tau)e^{-i\omega(t^{\prime}-\tau)}p(x^{\prime})\geq 0.

(H.64)

As before, the oscillatory terms cancel, the integral over
\tau
is proportional to the
\mathbb{G}_{0}(x,x^{\prime},\omega)
, i.e. the Fourier transform of
\mathbb{G}_{0}(x,x^{\prime},\tau)
, and the integral over
t^{\prime}
is simple to do. We are left with:

\displaystyle\frac{e^{2(\operatorname{Im}\omega)t}}{2\operatorname{Im}\omega}\operatorname{Im}\int\int{\rm d}x{\rm d}x^{\prime}\,p^{\dagger}(x)\left[\omega^{*}\mathbb{G}_{0}(x,x^{\prime},\omega)\right]p(x^{\prime})\geq 0.

(H.65)

If we again treat
\mathbb{G}_{0}(x,x^{\prime},\omega)
in space as a square matrix
\mathbb{G}_{0}(\omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\left[\omega^{*}\mathbb{G}_{0}(\omega)\right]\geq 0\quad\text{ for }\operatorname{Im}\omega>0,

(H.66)

where again the imaginary part of the matrix refers to its anti-Hermitian part.

Appendix I
Bounds on the coupling strengths of communication channels and their information capacities: supplementary

This appendix provides supplementary information to Chapter
6
“Bounds on the Coupling Strengths of Communication Channels and Their Information Capacities.”
We (1) present explicit expressions for the singular vectors of the Green’s function operator in the sphere–shell bounding volume and derive their corresponding singular values, (2) derive asymptotic form of the largest coupling strength in the limits of large bounding sphere and far-field bounding shell, (3) derive a lower bound on the total sum rule, and (4) derive an upper bound on the relative coupling strengths in the large-channel limit.

I.1 Singular values of the Green’s function operator in the sphere–shell bounding volume

In this section, starting from the full-electromagnetic wave equation, we define the dyadic Green’s function and expand it with spherical vector waves. The spherical vector waves are the singular vectors of the dyadic Green’s function operator in the sphere–shell bounding volume. We present explicit expressions for these singular vectors and derive their corresponding singular values. Results in this section supplement the arguments presented in Section
6.2.2
of Chapter
6
.

I.1.1
Dyadic Green’s function in spherical vector waves representation

The dyadic Green’s function
\bm{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})
in Chapter
6
is defined as the solution of the following wave equation under a point source excitation:

\frac{1}{k^{2}}\nabla\times{\nabla\times{\bm{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})}}-\bm{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=\mathbf{I}\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}),

(I.1)

where
\mathbf{I}
is the unit dyad and
k
is the magnitude of the free-space wavevector.
Slightly different from the conventional definition by a factor of
k^{2}
, the wave equation in Eq. (
I.1
) has the advantage of giving dimensionless singular values of the Green’s function operator since
\bm{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})
now has dimensions of reciprocal volume.
The solution of Eq. (
I.1
) is commonly written as
[
249
]

\bm{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=(k^{2}\mathbf{I}+\nabla\nabla)\frac{e^{ik|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}|}}{4\pi|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}|}.

(I.2)

Considering the spherical symmetry of the sphere–shell bounding volume, we express the dyadic Green’s function in Eq. (
I.2
) in its spherical vector waves representation
[
249
]
:

\bm{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=ik^{3}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-n}^{n}\sum_{j=1,2}\bm{v}_{{\rm out,}nmj}(\bm{x})\bm{v}_{{\rm reg,}nmj}^{*}(\bm{x}^{\prime}),

(I.3)

where
\bm{v}_{{\rm out,}nmj}(\bm{x})
and
\bm{v}_{{\rm reg,}nmj}(\bm{x}^{\prime})
are the outgoing and regular spherical vector waves. Their explicit expressions are discussed in the following subsection. The index
n
and
m
are the two indices of the underlying spherical harmonics, and
j=1,2
denotes the two possible polarizations of the transverse vector field.

I.1.2
Spherical vector waves as the singular vectors of the Green’s function operator

In this section, we give explicit expressions for the spherical vector waves,
\bm{v}_{{\rm out,}nmj}(\bm{x})
and
\bm{v}_{{\rm reg,}nmj}(\bm{x}^{\prime})
. We also present a crucial orthogonal relationship for these spherical vector waves, which allows us to identify them as the singular vectors of the Green’s function operator in the sphere–shell bounding volume.
The center of our sphere–shell bounding volume is chosen as the origin for the coordinates
\bm{x}
and
\bm{x}^{\prime}
.
In spherical coordinates
(r,\theta,\phi)
, the spherical vector waves can be separated into a radial dependency of a spherical Hankel/Bessel function and an angular dependency of vector spherical harmonics. Their spatial distributions depend the polarization state
j=\{1,2\}
, which we spell out separately:

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{{\rm out},nm1}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{n}h_{n}^{(1)}(kr)\mathbf{V}^{(3)}_{nm}(\theta,\phi)

(I.4)

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{{\rm out},nm2}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{n}\left\{n(n+1)\frac{h_{n}^{(1)}(kr)}{kr}\mathbf{V}^{(1)}_{nm}(\theta,\phi)+\frac{\left[krh_{n}^{(1)}(kr)\right]^{\prime}}{kr}\mathbf{V}^{(2)}_{nm}(\theta,\phi)\right\}

(I.5)

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{{\rm reg},nm1}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{n}j_{n}(kr)\mathbf{V}^{(3)}_{nm}(\theta,\phi)

(I.6)

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{{\rm reg},nm2}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{n}\left\{n(n+1)\frac{j_{n}(kr)}{kr}\mathbf{V}^{(1)}_{nm}(\theta,\phi)+\frac{\left[krj_{n}(kr)\right]^{\prime}}{kr}\mathbf{V}^{(2)}_{nm}(\theta,\phi)\right\},

(I.7)

where the prefactor
\gamma_{n}=1/\sqrt{n(n+1)}
.
The three vector spherical harmonics are an extension of the scalar spherical harmonics:
\mathbf{V}^{(1)}(\theta,\phi)=\hat{r}Y^{m}_{n}(\theta,\phi)
,
\mathbf{V}^{(2)}(\theta,\phi)=r\nabla\left[Y^{m}_{n}(\theta,\phi)\right]
, and
\mathbf{V}^{(3)}(\theta,\phi)=\nabla\times\left[\hat{r}Y^{m}_{n}(\theta,\phi)\right]
, where
Y_{n}^{m}(\theta,\phi)=\sqrt{\frac{(2n+1)(n-m)!}{4\pi(n+m)!}}P^{m}_{n}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi}
are the scalar spherical harmonics defined by associated Legendre polynomials
P^{m}_{n}(x)
. The radial dependency of the outgoing spherical vector harmonics in Eqs. (
I.4
,
I.5
) are the spherical Hankel function of the first kind,
h^{(1)}_{n}(kr)
, with the domain of
r
restricted to the region of the bounding shell. On the other hand, the regular spherical vector harmonics
\bm{v}_{{\rm reg,}nmj}(\bm{x})
are defined in the region of the bounding sphere. Because of this, their radial dependency follows the spherical Bessel function
j_{n}(kr)
. The regular spherical vector waves
\bm{v}_{{\rm reg,}nmj}(\bm{x})
in Eqs. (
I.6
,
I.7
) take the same forms as their outgoing counterparts
\bm{v}_{{\rm out,}nmj}(\bm{x})
but with every
h^{(1)}_{n}(kr)
replaced by
j_{n}(kr)
.

The three vector spherical harmonics,
\mathbf{V}_{nm}^{(1)}(\theta,\phi)
,
\mathbf{V}_{nm}^{(2)}(\theta,\phi)
, and
\mathbf{V}_{nm}^{(3)}(\theta,\phi)
, satisfy the following orthogonal property:

\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\sin\theta\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi\mathbf{V}_{nm}^{(\alpha)}(\theta,\phi)\cdot\mathbf{V}_{n^{\prime}m^{\prime}}^{(\beta)*}(\theta,\phi)=z_{\alpha n}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\delta_{nn^{\prime}},

(I.8)

where the prefactor
z_{1n}=1
and
z_{2n}=z_{3n}=n(n+1).

This orthogonality is crucial because it ensures that 1. different outgoing spherical vector waves
\bm{v}_{{\rm out},nmj}(\bm{x})
are orthogonal to each other in the outer bounding shell and 2. different regular spherical vector waves
\bm{v}_{{\rm reg},nmj}(\bm{x})
are orthogonal to each other in the inner bounding sphere.
Considering these two orthogonal conditions and the fact that the Green’s function operator in Eq. (
I.3
) can be expanded as the sum of the outer products between
\bm{v}_{{\rm out},nmj}(\bm{x})
and
\bm{v}_{{\rm reg},nmj}(\bm{x})
, we identify these two types of spherical vector waves as the left and right singular vectors of the Green’s function operator in the sphere–shell bounding volume.

The discussion of the spherical vector wave representation in this subsection mostly follows the presentation in Ref.
[
249
, chapter 2.1]
, though with different notations for the spherical vector waves and an extra factor of
k^{2}
in the Green’s function. We also adopt a more conventional definition of the scalar spherical harmonics as in Jackson
[
70
]
. This leads to different prefactors in Eqs. (
I.4
–
I.8
) compared to the ones in Ref.
[
249
]
.

I.1.3
Singular values of the Green’s function operator in the sphere–shell bounding volume

Given the spherical wave expansion of the Green’s function in Eq. (
I.3
), the singular values of the Green’s function operator in the sphere–shell bounding volume can be identified as the products between the norms of the unnormalized singular vectors
\bm{v}_{nmj}(\bm{x})
and
\text{Rg}\bm{v}_{nmj}(\bm{x})
in their respective domains:

|s_{nmj}^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}|^{2}=k^{6}\int_{V_{\text{sphere}}}\left|\bm{v}_{{\rm reg,}nmj}(\bm{x})\right|^{2}d\bm{x}\int_{V_{\text{shell}}}\left|\bm{v}_{{\rm out,}nmj}(\bm{x})\right|^{2}d\bm{x}.

(I.9)

The angular part of the integrals in Eq. (
I.9
) can be computed by plugging in the explicit expressions of
\bm{v}_{{\rm reg,}nmj}(\bm{x})
and
\bm{v}_{{\rm out,}nmj}(\bm{x})
in Eqs. (
I.4
–
I.7
), which are simplified under the orthogonality relation of the vector spherical harmonics
\mathbf{V}^{(i)}(\theta,\phi)
in Eq. (
I.8
).
The result is several remaining one-dimensional integrals in the radial direction:

\displaystyle|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{nm1}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int^{kR_{\text{sphere}}}_{0}x^{2}|j_{n}(x)|^{2}dx\int^{kR_{\text{outer}}}_{kR_{\text{inner}}}x^{2}|h^{(1)}_{n}(x)|^{2}dx

(I.10)

\displaystyle|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{nm2}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int^{kR_{\text{sphere}}}_{0}\left\{n(n+1)|j_{n}(x)|^{2}+\left[xj_{n}(x)\right]^{\prime\hskip 1.0pt2}\right\}dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\int^{kR_{\text{outer}}}_{kR_{\text{inner}}}\left\{n(n+1)|h^{(1)}_{n}(x)|^{2}+|xh^{(1)}_{n}(x)|^{\prime\hskip 1.0pt2}\right\}dx,

(I.11)

where
R_{\text{sphere}}
is the radius of the bounding sphere. The variables
R_{\text{inner}}
and
R_{\text{outer}}
are the inner and outer radii of the bounding shell, respectively.
The one-dimensional integrals in Eqs. (
I.10
,
I.11
) can be analytically integrated with the aid of indefinite integrals of the spherical Bessel functions in Ref.
[
333
]
, after which we obtain explicit expressions of the singular values in the sphere–shell bounding volume:

\displaystyle|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{nm1}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}}{16}x^{2}\left[J^{2}_{n+\frac{1}{2}}(x)-J_{n+\frac{3}{2}}(x)J_{n-\frac{1}{2}}(x)\right]\Biggr\rvert_{x=0}^{x=kR_{\text{sphere}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times y^{2}\operatorname{Re}\left[|H^{(1)}_{n+\frac{1}{2}}(x)|^{2}-H^{(1)}_{n+\frac{3}{2}}(y)H^{(2)}_{n-\frac{1}{2}}(y)\right]\Biggr\rvert_{y=kR_{\text{inner}}}^{y=kR_{\text{outer}}}

(I.12)

\displaystyle|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{nm2}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}}{16}x^{2}\left\{\frac{n+1}{2n+1}\left[J^{2}_{n-\frac{1}{2}}(x)-J_{n+\frac{1}{2}}(x)J_{n-\frac{3}{2}}(x)\right]\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\frac{n}{2n+1}\left[J^{2}_{n+\frac{3}{2}}(x)-J_{n+\frac{5}{2}}(x)J_{n+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\right]\right\}\Biggr\rvert_{x=0}^{x=kR_{\text{sphere}}}

\displaystyle\quad\times y^{2}\operatorname{Re}\left\{\frac{n+1}{2n+1}\left[|H^{(1)}_{n-\frac{1}{2}}(y)|^{2}-H^{(1)}_{n+\frac{1}{2}}(y)H^{(2)}_{n-\frac{3}{2}}(y)\right]\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\frac{n}{2n+1}\left[|H^{(1)}_{n+\frac{3}{2}}(y)|^{2}-H^{(1)}_{n+\frac{5}{2}}(y)H^{(2)}_{n+\frac{1}{2}}(y)\right]\right\}\Biggr\rvert_{y=kR_{\text{inner}}}^{y=kR_{\text{outer}}},

(I.13)

where the functions
J_{n}(x)
and
H^{(1)}_{n}(x)
denote the Bessel function and the Hankel function of the first kind. Equations (
I.12
,
I.13
) are the explicit expressions of
|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{nmj}|^{2}
we use in the Chapter
6
to calculate the upper bounds of the coupling strengths between any two regions in the bounding volume.

Figure I.1:
Numerical evidence showing that the maximum channel strength,
\max{|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{nmj}|^{2}}
, is asymptotically attained by the first angular channel of the second polarization state,
|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{002}|^{2}
, in the limit of large sphere radius
R_{s}
relative to the free-space wavelength
\lambda
.

I.2 Maximal channel strength in the limits of large bounding sphere and far-field bounding shells

We observe that the maximal channel strength in the sphere–shell bounding domain is asymptotically attained by the first angular channel of the second polarization state in the limit of large bounding sphere:

\max{|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{nmj}|^{2}}=|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{002}|^{2},\quad\text{for\ }R_{\rm sphere}\gg\lambda,

(I.14)

This is evidenced by Fig.
I.1
, which shows that the relative difference between
\max{|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{nmj}|^{2}}
and
|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{002}|^{2}
is smaller than 5% for a bounding sphere with radius
R_{\rm sphere}
larger than three times the wavelength
\lambda
and the relative difference asymptotically tends to zero as the radius becomes much larger than the wavelength. The separation distance
d
between the two bounding domains and the maximal thickness
2R_{r}
of the spherical shell is assumed to be
10\lambda
and
\lambda
, respectively, though our result does not appear to be sensitive to these two parameters.

The channel strength
|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{002}|^{2}
has a simple analytical form in the limits of large bounding sphere and far-field bounding shell. To show this, we first consider the limit of far-field bounding shell, where
|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{002}|^{2}
, according to Eq. (
I.11
), simplifies to

|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{002}|^{2}=2kR_{r}\int_{0}^{kR_{\rm sphere}}x^{2}|j_{-1}(x)|^{2}dx,\quad\text{for\ }d\gg\lambda,

(I.15)

where we approximate the spherical Hankel function as
h^{(1)}_{n}(x)\approx\frac{1}{x}e^{ix}i^{-n-1}
under the condition of
d\gg\lambda
. The integral in Eq. (
I.15
) can be analytically evaluated considering that
j_{-1}(x)=\cos(x)/x
. Its result, under the limit of large bounding sphere, further reduces to

|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{002}|^{2}=k^{2}R_{r}R_{\rm sphere},\ \text{for\ }R_{\rm sphere}\gg\lambda\text{\ and \ }d\gg\lambda.

(I.16)

Combining Eqs. (
I.14
) and (
I.16
), we derive an analytical expression of the maximal channel strength in the limits of large bounding sphere and far-field spherical shell:

\max{|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{nmj}|^{2}}=k^{2}R_{r}R_{\rm sphere},\ \text{for\ }R_{\rm sphere}\gg\lambda\text{\ and \ }d\gg\lambda,

(I.17)

which, we observe, scales linearly with the maximal radii of both the source and receiver domains.

I.3 A lower bound on the sum rule

The sum rule
S=\sum_{nmj}|s_{nmj}|^{2}
is conserved under a unitary transformation from the communication channel basis to the delta-function basis in real space. Conveniently, we express
S
as a double integral of the Frobenius norm of the dyadic Green’s function over both the source and receiver volumes:

S=\int_{V_{s}}\int_{V_{r}}||\bm{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})||_{F}^{2}d\bm{x}d\bm{x}^{\prime}.

(I.18)

The Frobenius norm of the dyadic Green’s function reads
[
61
]

||\bm{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})||_{F}^{2}=\frac{k^{6}}{8\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{(k|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}|)^{2}}+\frac{1}{(k|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}|)^{4}}+\frac{3}{(k|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}|)^{6}}\right]

(I.19)

which monotonically decays with respect to the separation distance,
|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}|
, between two points. This monotonic decay allows us to lower bound the sum rule by relaxing the separation distance to the largest possible separation distance,
\max{|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}|}=d+2R_{s}+2R_{r}
, between the source and receiver volumes:

S\geq\frac{k^{4}V_{s}V_{r}}{8\pi^{2}(d+2R_{s}+2R_{r})^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\left[k(d+2R_{s}+2R_{r})\right]^{-4}\right).

(I.20)

The variables
R_{s}
and
R_{r}
denote the maximal radii of the source and receiver domains.
For conciseness, we assume the furthest separated points are in the far field, i.e.
k(d+2R_{s}+2R_{r})\gg 1
, so that only the leading term in Eq. (
I.20
) remains. This, of course, can be easily generalized by explicitly including two other higher-order terms with a slightly more complicated expression.

I.4 An upper bound on the relative coupling strengths in the large-channel limit

In this section, we derive large-channel asympotes of the coupling strengths in the sphere–shell bounding volume.
Two differently polarized communication channels exhibit slightly different asymptotes, though both can be bounded above by a single expression.
Together with the lower bound on the total sum rule, we derive an upper bound on the relative coupling strength for both polarizations. This section provides a theoretical basis for the bound we present at the end of Section
6.2.2
of Chapter
6
.

The singular values
|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{nmj}|^{2}
have simple analytical expressions in the large-channel limit when the index
n\rightarrow\infty
. They can be derived by substituting the large-
n
asymptotes of the spherical Bessel and Hankel functions,
j_{n}(x)\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{(4n+2)x}}\left(\frac{ex}{2n+1}\right)^{n+1/2}
and
h^{(1)}_{n}(x)\sim\frac{-2i}{\sqrt{(4n+2)x}}\left(\frac{ex}{2n+1}\right)^{-n-1/2}
, into Eq. (
I.10
,
I.11
):

\displaystyle|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{nm1}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{kR_{\text{sphere}}}{2n}\right)^{4}\left(\frac{R_{\text{sphere}}}{R_{\text{inner}}}\right)^{2n-1}\quad\text{as\ }n\rightarrow\infty,

(I.21)

\displaystyle|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{nm2}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{R_{\text{sphere}}}{R_{\text{inner}}}\right)^{2n+1}\quad\text{as\ }n\rightarrow\infty.

(I.22)

While both polarizations decay exponentially as a function of
n
, the first polarization channel is always smaller than the second one in the large
n
limit due to the additional decay of the factor of
1/n^{4}
. The value of the second polarization thus serves as an upper bound for both:

|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{nmj}|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{R_{\text{sphere}}}{R_{\text{inner}}}\right)^{2n+1}\quad\text{as\ }n\rightarrow\infty.

(I.23)

This is an upper bound for the coupling strengths of both polarizations in a sphere–shell bounding volume in the large-channel limit. The bound only depends on the ratio between the radius of the bounding sphere,
R_{\text{sphere}}
, and the inner radius of the bounding shell,
R_{\text{inner}}
— the smaller the ratio, the faster the decay.

For any two domains that can be separated by a spherical surface, there are two possible sphere–shell bounding volumes: one that centers around the source region and one that centers around the receiver region. To obtain a tighter upper bound, we choose the one that centers around the smaller domain because it has the smaller ratio between
R_{\text{sphere}}
and
R_{\text{inner}}
. Considering this and the fact that the number of channels with
n
-index less or equal to
n
is
q=2(n+1)^{2}
, Eq. (
I.23
) can be written as

|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{q}|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{4}\left(1+\frac{R_{\text{min}}}{d}\right)^{-\sqrt{2q}-1}\quad\text{as\ }q\rightarrow\infty,

(I.24)

where
R_{\text{min}}=\text{\lx@text@lbrace}R_{s},R_{r}\}
is the smaller of the radius of the source domain
R_{s}
and the radius of the receiver domain
R_{r}
, and
d
is the distance between the two domains. This equation shows the coupling strengths between two regions always decay
sub
-exponentially with the total channel index,
q
, in the large-channel limit.

Lastly, we invoke the domain-monotonicity theorem discussed in Section
6.1
of Chapter
6
which implies that the coupling strengths
|s_{q}|^{2}
between any two domains have to be smaller than their counterparts in a sphere–shell bounding volume:

|s_{q}|^{2}\leq|s_{q}^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}|^{2},\quad\text{for}\ q=1,2,...

(I.25)

Combining this with the upper bound of
|s^{\text{(sphere--shell)}}_{q}|^{2}
in Eq. (
I.24
) and the lower bound of the sum rule
S
in Eq. (
I.20
), we derive an upper bound on the relative channel strengths in the large-channel limit:

\frac{|s_{q}|^{2}}{S}\leq\frac{2\pi^{2}(d+2R_{s}+2R_{r})^{2}}{k^{4}V_{s}V_{r}(1+d/R_{\text{min}})^{\sqrt{2q}+1}},\quad\text{as}\ q\rightarrow\infty.

(I.26)

This suggests that the relative coupling strength between any two domains decay at least sub-exponentially in the large-channel limit. Equation (
I.26
) is a key result presented in Chapter
6
and we hereby provide a derivation in this section.

Appendix J
Quick review of graph theory

Graphs distill real-world relations into abstract lines and vertices, such as social networks in sociology, genetic networks in biology, communication networks in computer science, lattice structures in physics, and molecular chains in chemistry. Complex dynamics untangle and straighten out on graphs.
In particular, chordal graphs represent a type of connections where many hard problems can be easily solved, including graph coloring, clique finding, and matrix factorization
[
199
]
.
In this appendix, we review basics of graphs and chordal graphs, as well as theorems that foreground chordal graphs in sparse semidefinite programming.

A graph composes of a set of vertices
V=\{v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{n}\}
and their connecting edges
E\subseteq V\times V
. The latter two uniquely define the graph:
G(V,E)
.
We consider undirectional graph whose edges are unordered pairs, denoted by curly brackets such as
\{v_{i},v_{j}\}
.
If a series of edges leads one vertex back to itself, then these edges form a cycle.
Two vertices
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
are adjacent if there is an edge between them, i.e., if
\{v_{i},v_{j}\}\in E
.
Vertices that are all adjacent to each other form a clique.
A clique can expand upon admitting new vertices that are adjacent to all its existing members. If no such new vertices exist, the clique is then called a maximal clique of the graph.

Many hard problems are easy to solve on a chordal graph. A chordal graph is a graph in which every cycle of length four and greater has a chord (an edge between nonconsecutive vertices of the cycle). In other words, if you trace a cycle of four (or more) edges without finding a shortcut, then the graph is not a chordal graph.
Any graph can be made chordal by adding extra edges. In the extreme case, supplying every possible edges guarantees a chordal graph, but the resulting graph has no sparsity left. The art is to add as few edges as possible to make a graph chordal. This procedure is called chordal completion, often implemented via heuristic algorithms
[
340
]
.

Graphs characterize sparsity in matrices. Specifically, each missing edge in an undirectional graph
G(V,E)
corresponds to two zeros in a symmetric matrix
\mathbb{X}
:
\{v_{i},v_{j}\}\notin E\iff\mathbb{X}_{ij}=\mathbb{X}_{ji}=0
.
Two groups of symmetric matrices
\mathbb{X}
are of particular interests in sparse semidefinite programming.
The first are matrices that are positive semidefinite and sparse, with sparsity pattern given by the graph
G(V,E)
:

\mathbb{S}_{+}^{n}(E,0)=\{\mathbb{X}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}_{+}\ |\ \mathbb{X}_{ij}=\mathbb{X}_{ji}=0\ \textrm{if}\ (i,j)\neq E\}.

(J.1)

The second are matrices that are not necessarily positive semidefinite or sparse but can be “completed” into positive semidefinite matrices. To motivate this, consider an inner product
\operatorname{Tr}(\mathbb{A}\mathbb{X})
between the symmetric matrix
\mathbb{X}
and a sparse symmetric matrix
\mathbb{A}
whose sparsity is given by the graph
G(V,E)
.
The inner product multiplies matrices element-wise, so only the elements in
\mathbb{X}
that correspond to the edges in the graph
G(V,E)
matter. If the rest of the elements in
\mathbb{X}
can be altered to turn
\mathbb{X}
into a positive semidefinite matrix, say
\mathbb{M}
, then we say
\mathbb{X}
belongs to a group of completable partial symmetric matrices:

\mathbb{S}_{+}^{n}(E,?)=\{\mathbb{X}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}\ |\ \exists\mathbb{M}\geq 0,\mathbb{M}_{ij}=\mathbb{X}_{ij}\ \forall(i,j)\in E\}

(J.2)

The two matrix spaces,
\mathbb{S}_{+}^{n}(E,0)
and
\mathbb{S}_{+}^{n}(E,?)
, are both convex cones and, in fact, dual of each other
[
199
]
. Together, they constitute the basic matrix spaces in sparse semidefinite programming.

Crucially, if the graph
G(E,V)
is a chordal graph, both types of matrices above can be decomposed into into smaller matrices defined on the maximal cliques of the graph:
\{C_{1},C_{2},...,C_{l}\}
.
Aiding this decomposition is a projection matrix
\mathbb{T}_{l}
that projects a matrix
\mathbb{X}
into its principle submatrix
\mathbb{X}_{l}=\mathbb{T}_{l}\mathbb{X}\mathbb{T}_{l}^{\top}
, and reads
(\mathbb{T}_{l})_{ij}=1
if
C_{l}(i)=j
and zero otherwise, where
C_{l}(i)
is the
i
-th vertex in
C_{l}
.
If the matrix
\mathbb{X}
is positive semidefinite completable, i.e.,
\mathbb{X}\in\mathbb{S}_{+}^{n}(E,?)
, then all the submatrices
\mathbb{X}_{l}
are all positive semidefinite, as dictated by the Grone’s theorem
[
341
]
:

\mathbb{X}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}_{+}(E,?)\iff\mathbb{X}_{l}=\mathbb{T}_{l}\mathbb{X}\mathbb{T}_{l}^{\top}\in\mathbb{S}^{|C_{l}|}_{+}.

(J.3)

If the matrix
\mathbb{X}
is sparse positive semidefinite, i.e.,
\mathbb{X}\in\mathbb{S}_{+}^{n}(E,0)
, then all the submatrices
\mathbb{X}_{l}
are positive semidefinite and uniquely expand the matrix
\mathbb{X}
, as dictated by Agler’s theorem
[
342
]
:

\mathbb{X}\in\mathbb{S}_{+}^{n}(E,0)\iff\mathbb{X}=\sum_{l=1}^{p}\mathbb{T}_{l}^{\top}\mathbb{X}_{l}\mathbb{T}_{l},\ \mathbb{X}_{l}\in\mathbb{S}^{|C_{l}|}_{+}.

(J.4)

Agler’s theorem in Eq. (
J.4
) allows one to decompose sparse semidefinite programs in their dual forms, where the matrix variable
\mathbb{X}
directly inherits the sparsity of the problem; Grone’s theorem in Eq. (
J.3
) allows one to decompose sparse semidefinite programs in their primal forms, where the the matrix variable
\mathbb{X}
does not inherit the sparsity but is multiplied by matrices that do.
